#6832. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6831. Women’s Sports Teams Deserve Better Than What Their Homes Are Giving Them

Apparently, we’re going through this...again!

Gotham FC, the current reigning National Women’s Soccer Team champions, are on their way to winning another title. But their journey—which includes the chance to host their first semifinal game at home—has come with a major surprise: a change of venues. After their quarterfinal match against the Seattle Reign FC ended with a messed-up pitch and a fine against Gotham FC due to its conditions, the team was forced to find another place for their next match. The bigger issue? Their only option is a 5,000-person stadium located in Randall’s Island, New York.

Over 11,000 people made their way to Sports Illustrated Stadium—the home to Gotham FC as well as the MLS’s New York Red Bulls—to see them take down Seattle Reign FC. This means that less than half of that audience will be able to catch them at the match that can lead them to the finals.

While pitch conditions are absolutely important for the safety of the players and a big enough reason to change venues, the option to upgrade the pitch to a hybrid model was shut down by none other than New York Red Bulls due to a friendly game—AKA a game that does not count towards their season—they have on Saturday. Although the pitch would be ready for Gotham FC’s big game, RBNY shut down the plan as it wouldn’t be ready for their planned friendly. All of a sudden, a game-making match for a winning women’s team was being moved to a much smaller space due to a men’s game that does not count towards anything.

If you’re getting a sense of deja vu, that’s because this is not the first time a women’s team had to change their plans due to a men’s team. The WNBA’s Atlanta Dream were forced to move one of their WNBA Finals games from 8 p.m. to 12:30 p.m. on a Thursday. While the move was caused by them switching venues—going from their home at the Gateway Center, which fits 3,500 to the State Farm Arena which seats 17,000—it was also caused by a scheduled Atlanta Hawks game, a team that hasn’t won an NBA title since the 1950s.

It’s clear that no matter where they go, these women’s teams’ homes are not giving them what they need: a space where they can continue to host their ever-growing fanbases without fear of having to fall to the needs of a men’s team. The WNBA had a 21 percent increase in attendance to their games in 2026. The NWSL also gained some new faces in the crowd with a 3 percent increase, according to Sports Business Journal.

In 2028, Gotham FC will move to Etihad Park, a new stadium that will hold 25,000 fans in Queens, NYC. However, they’ll still have the problem of sharing the field with New York City FC, meaning this problem can always come up again.

A clear solution would be to give these incredible teams their own venues, but it’s evident that, financially, building a new stadium is not the easiest for some of these teams who are still growing. But, maybe next time, these men’s teams will just get the hint and let the real winners take over. It’s their home, too, after all.

#6830. The 17 Best Comedy Movies of 2026

Thanks to The Invite, Coyote vs. Acme, and Stop! That! Train!, big-screen comedy is making a comeback.

Are comedies making a comeback? Over the past decade, you couldn’t get a laugh into theaters even if it was forced. Hollywood decided that comedies weren’t worth the same treatment as superhero tentpoles or low-budget horror, and just about every legacy franchise sequel of a former ‘90s comedy disappointed audiences because they were basically cash grabs that played on nostalgia. Comedy needed help.

So, in 2026, some fresh films forced Hollywood to come to its senses. Coyote vs. Acme, a Looney Tunes film that Warner Bros. originally dropped to claim a tax write-off, grossed over $126 million at the box office. Olivia Wilde’s The Invite might just receive nominations for every major award category at the Oscars, and Airplane!-esque humor made a comeback in RuPaul’s hilarious disaster flick Stop! That! Train!

If you’re in need of a belly laugh, check out the best comedies of 2026 below.

Don’t get me wrong, Coyote vs. Acme is no Space Jam. But the Looney Tunes film that triumphantly won the box office—even after Warner Bros. gave up on it—is still one of the funniest movies of the year. The story follows Wile E. Coyote as he partners with a lowly injury attorney (Will Forte) to sue the ACME Corporation for its defective products. The comedy also stars John Cena as ACME’s defense attorney, who probably has the funniest line of the year when he tells Tweety Bird: “I don’t pay you to tink you taw, I pay you to taw.”

Olivia Colman stars in this romantic comedy drama about a lonely fisherwoman who grows tired of the town’s harassment due to her smell. So, she asks a magical basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to make her a husband (Alexander Skarsgård) out of wicker. What follows is a bizarre yet hilarious story about the follies of marriage, jealousy, and tons of sex with a man made out of basket material.

Sam Rockwell leads this sci-fi comedy by former Pirates of the Caribbean director Gore Verbinski, about a time-traveler who travels to the same diner over and over again in a Groundhog Day–esque loop because he believes that if he can take the right configuration of people from the diner back to the future with him then he may be able to save the world from AI’s takeover. I know, it’s strange. But Verbinski makes the comedy work, especially when you learn about each diner patron via small vignettes emphasizing various doomsday scenarios.

Real estate agent Rudd Landy (John Cena) is called to the hospital when his brother gets into a car accident. Turns out, the man in emergency care is not his real brother (Christopher Meloni), but someone that he used to mentor when he was younger as part of a Big Brothers Big Sisters program. Without anywhere else to go, Rudd agrees to help take care of his “little brother” (Eric André) while he recovers, even if his little brother might just ruin his life.

When Gail Daughtry’s (Zoey Deutch) husband takes their celebrity sex pass joke literally, Gail sets out to get even by tracking down her celebrity sex pass—John Hamm—to have sex with him and even out the score. The comedy film also stars “Weird Al” Yankovic, Ben Wang, Sabrina Impacciatore, Joe Lo Truglio, Fred Melamed, and Mad Men’s John Slattery as himself.

Johnny Knoxville, Steve-O, and the whole Jackass crew return for one last ride in Jackass: Best and Last, another compilation of skits, stunts, and pranks from some of the most fearless people on the planet. They hit each other in the groin with wooden mallets, hook their penises up to shock collars, and reminisce about some of their most iconic sketches throughout the years.

Half RuPaul’s Drag Race, half Airplane! for Bravo fans, Stop! That! Train! is the dumbest movie I’ve seen all year (complimentary). The film follows RuPaul and her Drag Race stars as they attempt to stop a speeding bullet train heading straight toward the storm of the century, aka a Stormaganza. Even if you’re so out of your depth that you have no idea what’s going on, Stop! That! Train! argues that that’s okay. It’s comedy for comedy’s sake, and we need more of that at the theater.

Mel Brooks just turned 100 years old (!!!). But before that, he was 99 in Mel Brooks: The 99-Year-Old Man!—a two-part HBO documentary directed by Judd Apatow. The film, which chronicles the comedian’s career throughout The Producers, Blazing Saddles, Spaceballs, Young Frankenstein, and many more, also touches on his work as a film producer. Still, it’s the testimonies from his friendship with Carl Reiner and his marriage to Anne Bancroft that really pull at the heartstrings after a good laugh down memory lane.

Olivia Wilde is not afraid of Virginia Woolf. In the actress’s latest film, The Invite, the classic dinner-party setup full of playful talking and relationship arguments somehow morphs into a sex comedy. The whole film takes place in Angela (Wilde) and Joe’s (Seth Rogen) apartment as they welcome the raucous upstairs neighbors Pina (Penelope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton). But unlike Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf? and its descent into anger and grief, The Invite asks more questions about love, commitment, and everyone’s stance on orgies.

The struggling music duo always works for me. Whether it’s Flight of the Conchords or Ishtar, there’s something funny about people with no talent still trying to make it to the top. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie takes the genre to maybe its lowest budget yet, as the Canadian web-series comedians somehow also make room to spoof Back to the Future and incorporate real Nathan Fielder–like guerrilla filmmaking.

One of the coolest movies to premiere out of SXSW was the Hulu original Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice, from writer/director BenDavid Grabinski. A loan shark (Vince Vaughn) uses a time machine to undo one bad night involving his close friend (James Marsden) and his wife (Eiza González). Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice is the kind of movie you want when the pizza is hot, the beers are cold, and your friends are indecisive about what you all want to watch.

After years of creative and financial slump, Pixar has found its groove again with Hoppers. Essentially Animal Farm meets Avatar, Hoppers tells the story of a genius college student whose participation in a breakthrough science experiment lets her become a beaver who can communicate with animals. Though her efforts to help animals seem promising at first, she quickly realizes what really happens when animals (and bugs!) fight back against mankind. Directed by We Bare Bears creator Daniel Chong, Hoppers is an easygoing, family-friendly romp through the wilderness.

Liam Neeson continues his funny streak in the action comedy Cold Storage. Joe Keery (Stranger Things) plays a slacker employee of a storage facility who, along with his cute coworker (Georgina Campbell), discovers a deadly, mutating fungus. Teaming up with a grizzled federal agent (Neeson), the three work to save the world. Basically a modern-day Outbreak, Cold Storage isn’t one to leave dormant on your watch list.

Dave Bautista and Jason Momoa make up the ultimate action-movie tag team in The Wrecking Crew. The guys play estranged brothers in Hawaii who work together to piece together who killed their father and how on earth the Japanese yakuza is involved.

Boots Riley, the writer and director of Sorry to Bother You, does it again with his crime caper I Love Boosters. In this anticapitalist satire, a crew of professional thieves works to take down a ruthless fashion giant (Demi Moore). Keke Palmer, Naomi Ackie, Taylour Paige, Poppy Liu, Eiza González, and more make up this delirious ensemble that takes Ocean’s Eleven and dresses it in hyper-surreal shades.

Sometimes we need a nauseatingly cute rom-com. Kevin James (yes, The King of Queens) stars in this breezy and uplifting comedy about a man who, after being left at the altar, goes on his Italian honeymoon solo. While there, he makes new friends and even deeper connections with strangers halfway around the world. A feel-good flick about new beginnings, Solo Mio can lift you high when you’re feeling down.

A stoner comedy? In 2026? In this economy? Well, the streaming generation can finally know what it’s like to have their own Harold & Kumar with Pizza Movie. Gaten Matarazzo and Sean Giambrone star as college roommates and social pariahs who, while high on a new hallucinogenic drug, embark on a psychedelic odyssey to make it downstairs and get their pizza delivery. Despite a popular meme mocking the movie’s marketing, Pizza Movie performed well with critics at its SXSW premiere.

#6828. 63 Gifts for Tween Girls That'll Make You the Cool Grown-Up

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Here's what's on their wish list... for now, at least.

Oh, you think shopping for a teenager is rough these days? Try figuring out what’s still in style among the 9- to 12-year-old set. While you could play it safe and stock them up on so much viral TikTok stuff, you run the risk of picking something that’s no longer “cool”—tween whims seem to change with zero notice to anyone over the age of 21. But never fear: our gift guide editors on top of it!

We spend our days poring over beauty samples, viral tech gadgets, and the latest fashion trends that have captured the attention of the average tween. If she's seen it on TikTok, we've probably tried it too. With the holidays around the corner, use our edit to surprise her with a special gift.

Whether she's redecorating her room for the third time or amassing a beauty collection that's bigger than yours, we found the gifts that’ll make her think you're a mind reader.

At under $50, this custom name bracelet makes a thoughtful gift. Choose a single or double strand, and add up to nine letters. If there’s space, you can add a heart, star, peace sign, or plus sign.

ICYMI, everyone loves the lululemon Everywhere Belt Bag, but not everyone knows that it comes in a clear version. It's perfect for field trips, concerts, or other events that require see-through bags.

She may not be wearing perfume every day, but for special occasions, she'll love reaching for this set. The all-ages scents are fruity and floral, and they’ll look beyond cute on her dresser or vanity.

These platform Uggs have been a must-have for the past few winters, and they’re still at the top of plenty of wish lists. She’ll love the plush wool-blend lining, the braided collar, and the sturdy sole she can wear to school and everywhere else.

She can focus on homework, stream her favorite shows, or write in her journal, all from the comfort of her bed, thanks to this pillow that lets her sit upright comfortably.

#6827. I Can Feel the Tremors of History Rumbling Beneath Scott Colom’s Victory

The senator-elect from Mississippi invoked Medger Evers in his winning speech, which reminded me of how far we’ve come—and how far we still have to go.

(Optional Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Okay! Now we can celebrate. From the Mississippi Free Press:

Oh, to hell with the numbers. Let’s just celebrate a win by a guy cool enough to receive the good news of his election while wearing a Richard Pryor hoodie. From his victory speech:

One day in 1994, in the employ of another magazine, I went down to Jackson on assignment for a story about the death penalty that ultimately never ran. It was the day after the Republicans, led by Representative Newt Gingrich, won control of the entire Congress for the first time since 1952 and control of the House of Representatives for the first time since 1954. It can be argued, and I have, that these results scared the courage out of the Democratic party as thoroughly as George McGovern’s landslide loss to Richard Nixon in 1972 did. And when the modern Democratic party gets scared, it inevitably runs away from its most sacred commitments and the proudest moments in its history. This result caused Democratic president Bill Clinton to sign grotesque legislation like the Defense of Marriage Act and a crime bill that, relevant to my mission to Mississippi, added 28 new death-penalty crimes to the federal code. The bar in the Walthall Hotel, where I was staying, was filled every night by gloating Republicans, which drove me to room service for the balance of my stay.

On my last day in Jackson, I took a drive across town to what was then called 2332 Guynes Street in the Elraine subdivision, which had been developed in the 1950s by men named Winston Thompson and Leroy Burnett in order to service the rising Black middle class of the city. One of the houses in the development was purchased by Medgar and Myrlie Evers. On the night of June 12, 1963, Myrlie and their children were watching the speech in which President John F. Kennedy finally got off the dime on civil rights when they heard Medgar’s car coming up the driveway. The kids ran out to greet him. From behind a bush across the street, a back-shooting coward named Byron De La Beckwith sighted down his .30–06 Enfield and murdered Medgar Evers in front of his family.

I parked my car and walked slowly from De La Beckwith’s sniper’s nest to the driveway at 2332 Guynes Street, tracing the route of the bullet that killed Medgar Evers on that night in 1963. I was strangely aware of every step I took. I was deeply aware of the rise and fall of my feet, one after the other. It was as though there were a tiny tremor of history beneath me the closer I came to the spot under the carport where he fell, bleeding, before his family. It felt very much like a pilgrimage to some ancient shrine. There were sad and solemn bells tolling in my mind.

Listening to Scott Colom accept his victory on Wednesday night, as he name-checked Medgar Evers, along with Fannie Lou Hamer and Hiram Revels, I heard those bells again in my mind. Loud and raucous this time, they drowned out the crack of the whip and the crack of a .30–06 Enfield rifle fired with murderous intent. This time, they rang with joy, and the tremors of history I felt beneath my feet that day in 1994 felt like an earthquake.

#6824. No More Franchises

No more prequels. No more reboots. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more of these 71 other things.

No more movie franchises. No more shared universes. No more post-credits scenes. No more prequels. No more reboots. No more eight-hour streaming TV spin-offs. No more slates and phases. No more multiverses. No more crossovers.

No more movies that are only made to greenlight sequels. No more sequels made for getting you to sign up for a streamer that raises its monthly subscription price every year. No more Easter eggs. No more winks. No more fourth-wall breaks. No more action scenes that halt the story and are clearly directed by the second unit. No more teasers that lead to nothing. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more watch guides and chronological order explainers and breakdowns. No more asking, “Is it canon?”

No more four-quadrant tentpoles. No more movies based on kids’ stuff that my niece can’t actually watch. No more Superman movies that are so violent and destructive that they trigger my 9/11 survivor mother’s PTSD. No more R-rated films that sell Funkos. No more movies that have to appeal to foreign markets and all the meemaws and pawpaws in Idaho. No more quippy dialogue where characters remark, “Well, THAT just happened!” No more irony. No more grotesque violence set to upbeat pop songs. No more characters looking into their own phones. No more thudding exposition, and especially not in the first ten minutes. No more “no more romances.”

No more hiring interesting up-and-coming filmmakers with buzz coming out of Sundance and Telluride because they’re cheap and have credibility to exploit. No more boxing in those same artistes because they’ll say “yes” to all marching orders. No more rejecting established auteurs with an interesting pitch and a desire to play in the sandbox because it doesn’t fit into next summer’s tentpole. No more prioritizing release dates over craft and the well-being of everyone working on it.

No more geriatric thespians putting on spandex because today’s adult moviegoer refuses to grow up themselves. No more casting red-hot twenty-somethings and locking them into seven-movie contracts. No more firing those actors because they got into legal trouble so the audience gets gaslit into forgetting there was ever something called The Kang Dynasty. No more international talent getting nothing cool to do; no more Hiroyuki Sanada getting killed by Jeremy Renner in Avengers: Endgame. No more casting directors deciding based on their exact amount of Instagram followers. No more content creators getting cameos.

No more Comic-Con. Or rather, no more making Comic-Con into Coachella. No more sleeping on the sidewalks for a seat in Hall H. No more buzzy presentations that turn art and myth into product reveals. No more “we’re doing it for the fans” and “one more thing.” No more Oscar winners promoting movies that don’t even have a script. No more actors lying through their teeth about how they read the original comics growing up. No more trailers that shake the room for those who slept outside to get in, and then get uploaded to YouTube while they collect their sleeping bags.

No more press screenings with ridiculous embargoes. No more taking away my phone and stuffing it into a zip pouch because of a spoiler that heaven forbid leaks on TikTok when millions were spent making it in front of IMAX cameras and a unionized crew; no more thinking any of this is tantamount to government secrets. No more influencers on red carpets. No more junket “interviews” that are just quizzes and games. No more gushing. No more unprofessionalism.

No more studios that exist only to adapt in-house IP. No more C-suites rambling about the lucrative potential in old cartoons and ‘90s comic books like old-timey oil barons surveying land. No more touting the box office of one movie as evidence why they oughta make a dozen more. No more pretenses about the power of stories in earnings calls with Merrill Lynch. No more forgetting, and not giving healthy royalties to, the comic book creators whose imaginations filled the calendar slate and fueled the stock prices in the first place.

No more toxic fandoms. No more powerful studios kowtowing to the loudest, most obnoxious YouTubers who make a living screaming into microphones in their basements. No more chuds who bemoan wokeness in whatever the latest remake/sequel they’re supposed to be mad about, and use AI-generated thumbnails of themselves making the dumbest fucking faces with cherry-picked unflattering screenshots of actresses who wouldn’t give them the time of day anyway. No more abusers, sex pests, misogynists, and racists anywhere, let alone in charge.

No more ten-year plans. No more stoking tuckered-out fans to drum up excitement about the next Batman or James Bond. No more actors keeping up action figure physiques while they wait years to hear about the sequel. No more taking characters and stories people have spent their lives escaping into for comfort and deforming them into numbers on a spreadsheet. No more vague bullshit like “It didn’t capture the zeitgeist” to explain why you’re arm-swiping everything off the table again. No more using the word “zeitgeist” if you cannot define it without ChatGPT.

No more cowardice. No more promises no one can keep. No more of this circus. No more.

#6820. A Behind-the-Scenes Look at Esquire’s Party for the 20th Anniversary of the Big Black Book

At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, a bunch of New York’s coolest people gathered to raise a glass to the essential guide to style and luxury.

It’s been two decades since the first edition of the Big Black Book debuted in 2006. Back then, a style guide devoted to the ins and outs of the luxury market wasn’t exactly common, but we believed it was necessary. The idea wasn’t to spam readers with a bunch of stuff they couldn’t afford but to give them a glimpse into the world of well-made, time-tested pieces that can create the backbone of a considered wardrobe.

Turns out, we were right. Men want to dress well, and they want to invest in the items—bench-made boots, bespoke tailoring, designer apparel—that look great on day one but even better a few years down the line. The fact that the Big Black Book is still going strong in 2026 is a testament to that, so we decided to celebrate. At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, we gathered a group of people who represent the BBB ethos. Sharp dressers, sure, but also sharp minds—the folks who don’t just define luxury in the moment but also lay the foundation for its future.

It would’ve been even more fun to invite every Big Black Book fan along for the ride, but we quickly realized that logistics—and fire marshals—might not allow it. So, in lieu of bringing everyone inside, we’re sharing a gallery of photos from the party. Keep scrolling for an inside look at the festivities.

#6817. Make the Skirt Your Fall Personality

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Thanks to Prada, the skirt has never felt fresher

The collection seen around the world this year was, by all accounts, Prada’s Spring/Summer 2027 show, which served as something of an edict on the skirt. Composed of 63 looks, each featuring a distinct iteration in varying colors and fabrications, the presentation was nothing short of a skirt master class from Miuccia Prada. Following that spectacle, you would have been hard-pressed not to spot a skirt on nearly every runway, be it the gold-leaf pencil skirts at Saint Laurent or the many offerings from Julian Klausner’s Dries Van Noten, which brought to light another unsung hero as of late: the ocelot print!

Though the silhouette felt decidedly new under the glamour of the spring collections, the truth of the matter is that skirts made a similar splash during the fall shows. However, the garment itself wasn’t necessarily the prevailing message. Rather, fall/winter 2026 signaled a return to a more considered approach to dressing, with perhaps no better champion of this sensibility than Matthieu Blazy at Chanel, whose interpretation of the wardrobe staple helped make it feel novel once again.

Of course, as temperatures drop, the natural inclination is to reach for clothing that provides maximum coverage, which often means an onslaught of pants, whether in heavy wool or denim. However, all hope is not lost for the garment of the moment as we inch towards brisker weather. The key to making it work during the colder months comes down to a thoughtful approach to layering, as well as opting for styles in substantial fabrics that can stand up to the chill.

Below, you’ll find eight skirt styles, like those in leather or denim, and how to wear them for everything from weekend adventures to boardroom meetings without feeling too buttoned-up.

Perhaps Matthieu Blazy’s design prowess could be likened to the Midas touch, because just as he has helped bring the skirt back into relevance, so too has he turned to the quarter-zip. Channel a bit of Chanel’s Métiers d’Art 2026 collection by pairing an oatmeal-colored cashmere-blend quarter-zip with a tartan skirt adorned with fringe, and introduce some daytime drama with a pair of shoulder-duster earrings. To finish things off, reach for croc-embossed Mary Jane pumps and a stark white shoulder bag for just the right amount of ladies-who-lunch panache.

Guest in Residence Tartan Wrap Fringed-Detail Skirt

Loewe Scarf Mini Knotted Leather Shoulder Bag

One of the great pleasures of fall is venturing outside the city for some much-needed fresh country air. Channel a more pastoral approach to dressing with a long, box-pleated corduroy skirt paired with a lightweight cashmere turtleneck and a plaid shirt. Top everything off with a cropped puffer jacket featuring an aviator-inspired shearling collar, a detail echoed in the retro sensibility of a pair of shield sunglasses.

Tom Ford Bronson Acetate Pilot Sunglasses

Polo Ralph Lauren Matte Woven Down Bomber Jacket

Though Western dressing seems to be an ever-present trend, there’s no denying that the aesthetic has become increasingly dominant in recent seasons. To channel it without looking like an extra in a rodeo show, the key is to mix hallmarks of the style, such as suede and turquoise accents, be it in the form of a concho belt or jewelry, with more everyday pieces. Consider a vintage-inspired gray hoodie and croc-embossed high-shaft boots that evoke the old West without feeling overwrought. Finish with a cropped bomber jacket in a similar hue to the skirt to create the impression of a matching set.

Isabel Marant Étoile Belma Faux Leather Jacket

Banana Republic x La Ligne Suede Wrap Midi Skirt

When it comes to fall dressing, it’s quite natural to gravitate toward a palette of muted tones and neutrals, perhaps punctuated by an interesting pop of red or even the color of the season, purple. However, don’t be afraid to go against the grain and embrace a more saturated palette, particularly with holiday gatherings on the horizon. Consider this hostess-ready taffeta ball skirt in a shocking pinkish-red hue, paired with a pink button-down shirt and a studded enamel starfish ring set with peridot. To temper the abundance of color, introduce touches of white in the form of a topcoat and retro-inspired kitten heels finished with a bow, lending the outfit a welcome sense of balance.

The Frankie Shop Gaia Double-Breasted Coat

Give the traditional prep-school uniform a contemporary twist by pairing an A-line denim skirt with a striped long-sleeve polo shirt and an unconventional gray wool blazer with double lapels. Instead of the typical boot, consider opting for the trendy optic-white jazz shoe for an unexpected touch. For some added visual interest and warmth, introduce a scarf in a complementary hue.

A leather skirt can, at times, feel a bit severe, so consider softening its appearance with your go-to white T-shirt and a V-neck sweater in a complementary shade of gray. For a downtown cool factor, reach for a pair of slip-on loafers in lieu of heels or a boot. Finish with a statement cuff for added polish.

Majestic Filatures Cotton Crewneck T-Shirt

Office attire has become considerably more relaxed in recent years, letting personal style shine. A utilitarian skirt turns traditional workwear on its head, but pair it with familiar staples, such as a striped monk-collar shirt and a knitted vest, both rendered in unexpected color combinations. Finish with a herringbone overcoat and second-skin wine-colored booties for a stylish ensemble that feels anything but corporate.

Jamie Haller Striped Cotton and Silk Blend Poplin Shirt

Dries Van Noten Toner Wool Contrast V-Neck Top

In recent seasons, we’ve seen a variety of animal prints come into play, well beyond the ubiquitous leopard, making room for everything from zebra and ocelot to tiger. These patterns are statement makers on their own, which is precisely why they work so well next to understated pieces. Consider styling them with a black short-sleeved cashmere T-shirt and wool cardigan, which allows the print to command attention without overwhelming. Add a waxed-cotton jacket and riding boots for an interesting counterpoint that feels both practical and playful.

#6812. The Audacious Southern Chef Teaching Angelenos to Shut Up and Listen

Vinyl bars like West Hollywood’s Darling, from star chef Sean Brock, will help you to appreciate music, and food, and life, all over again.

In our increasingly soulless world of algorithmic playlists and AI-generated art, one of life’s great analog pleasures is listening to records with friends. A dusty old LP pre-breaks the ice for new connections, unlocks stories you haven’t heard from old ones, gives you the chance to get collectively lost in the music.

Vinyl night is such a vital human need that it’s a surprise there aren’t more third spaces built around satisfying it. Characteristically, Japan is miles ahead of America on this front. In the country’s countless kissa bars, bartenders mix drinks and spin records, and—I swear this is true—patrons keep their voices down and listen. My partner and I saw and heard it for ourselves on a trip to Japan last year: Every major city has a few joints where the speakers are big, the tables have signs that say NO LOUD LAUGHING, and the customers obey. At a packed and reverently silent bar in Kyoto called PM Sounds, a few whiskies deep and well into the a.m., side one of Jeff Buckley’s Grace hit just right. “Did we really fly halfway around the world to sit and listen to an album we have at home?” I asked Ben on the walk back to the hotel. “Yes,” he replied, and we both agreed it was worth the trip.

Americans are famously not a quiet people, nor are we particularly good with being told what to do. Could kissa culture happen here? A few places in Los Angeles are rising to the challenge: The Gold Line in Highland Park has a hip crowd and shelves swelling with the massive record collection of owner Peanut Butter Wolf. Hollywood’s Living Room is massive and meticulously acoustically treated, and it’s currently one of the tightest doors in town. But in West Hollywood, across the street from metastasizing gay club the Abbey and steps away from the log cabin where the hip AA meetings happen, southern super chef Sean Brock just might have hit the sweet spot: His first California restaurant, Darling, has an eclectic menu, outstanding cocktails, and speakers that will make you feel like you’re right there in the recording studio. “Music brings people together, just like food,” Brock says. “I’ve been obsessed with listening bars for a while, and I’ve been trying to get people into them since 2023. The thrill of discovery is amazing, and I think the more AI the world gets, the more we’re going to reach for this stuff to balance it out.”

Brock looks every inch both the 21st-century Generation X chef and DJ: horn-rimmed glasses, sleeve tattoos, ADHD energy. He’s the James Beard Award–winning chef behind a string of beloved Nashville restaurants past and present, including modern southern standby Husk and two-in-one restaurants Audrey and June. He’s respected by foodies and the hoi polloi alike; ask anyone who did time in Nashville this century and they’ll tell you a snack at the bar of a Sean Brock joint is the reward for a hard day’s work. When we first met, just before Darling opened, we bonded over his massive collection of 45’s in the DJ booth that looks over the restaurant’s bar area. As a secular song from New Orleans preacher Bobby Powell faded out, he wanted to play me something. “Have you heard this?” It was a cover of the Mamas and the Papas’ “California Dreamin’ ” by a band from Jackson, Tennessee, called the Jacksonians. “This is all they ever did! They teased us with this and then never did another thing!” As it played, we paused, we nodded our heads. “This is the one. When people hear this, they stop talking.”

The DJ booth and its speaker cabinets were made custom for the space. “The key is the wood,” he said. “Thirteen-ply Baltic birch, which is impossible to get now.” Within the cabinets: massive Italian speakers, with Altec Lansing drivers upcycled from their first life in movie theaters.

When Brock is at Darling—generally on the weekdays if he’s not traveling—he plays records as the restaurant opens, takes over in the kitchen as the dinner rush starts, then hops back onto the ones and twos after the last dish has been served, going as long into the night as the city will let him. “Getting to play music for people after working in the kitchen all night is my reward now. For the longest time, it was getting a beer after work. Now it’s just watching music bring people happiness.”

As a record collector and a chef, Brock is always thinking about the evolution of local flavor. “What I’m chasing is the knowledge of the sound and the rhythm of a city and a time,” he told me. Take Memphis in the early ’70s, for example. “There was Willie Mitchell at Royal Studios with the Hi label doing Al Green and Ann Peebles, and you had Stax. And then there was this anger building because of the Vietnam War, people are upset, and you feel that in the music.” He grabbed a 7-inch from the pile, the gateway 45 that got him into record collecting: Blackrock’s “Yeah Yeah.” And I did feel it, because the band is a Murderers’ Row of ’70s Memphis session musicians, but mostly because of those speakers. At the right volume and in the right seat, the T. rex from Jurassic Park has nothing on Blackrock.

At its outset, Brock intended Darling to give him a new identity away from the southern cuisine that made him famous. “I didn’t want to come here and cook a bunch of stuff I’d already cooked,” he said. “And we’d been told L.A. wasn’t interested in southern cooking, that it would be too heavy or whatever. So we didn’t cook any southern dishes when we opened.” The next time I stopped in, once Darling had been up and running for a few months, that whole idea had been 86’d. “About three weeks in, everybody was like: Where are the grits, Sean?

“Now this has turned into a place where people come to taste the flavors of the South that I’ve been working on for 20 years—all the seed saving, all the research on traditions and recipes and history. I had gotten a little bored with it, honestly, but all of a sudden I have a brand-new audience for whom it’s completely new, and I’m completely reinvigorated.”

He was describing, almost exactly, the experience Ben and I had hearing Jeff Buckley in Japan. Whether it’s food or music, there’s something revelatory about paying close attention, even to something you think you know.

So that’s the food, and I can tell you from experience that the steak burger is worth the trip (and make that trip early; he makes only 24 a night). As for the music, Brock believes in the power of the listening bar with the zeal of the recent kissa convert. “I had a kind of life-changing moment in a listening bar around 2022,” he says. “I was sitting in the sweet spot—the tip of the triangle, equidistant from two gigantic Tannoys, and the sound became 3D. It floated. I stopped drinking nine years ago, but I thought they’d accidentally given me alcohol.” He got on Nashville Craigslist that very night, searched “Tannoy,” and sent a message to a seller, who wrote back, “I ate in your restaurant last night.” It’s good to be the king.

Brock has seats in the bar area of Darling situated at the perfect distance and angle from the speakers to give customers an out-of-body experience if they want it. But like a legendary southern chef distancing himself from grits, a listening bar in Los Angeles is a risky concept. In Japan, the locals and tourists know to keep quiet and focus on the music, but this is West Hollywood, where everybody wants to be the thing that is focused on. Where if people have a song selector they trust, it’s usually their Peloton instructor. As an Angeleno, I have to worry whether anyone will pipe down long enough to pay attention. “There’s a difference between a night where you come to eat and drink and socialize while music is playing and a night where we’re saying: What we’re playing is special and rare. I want to test that theory soon,” Brock told me. He’s been promoting more and more listening sessions: tributes to jazz record labels Strata-East and International Anthem; a vinyl party hosted by Sylvan Esso; Dolly Parton’s first 12 albums, in order, on those THX-tested speakers.

Even the untrained ear can hear it coming together. I swing through again to hear local legend and NTS radio host DJ Morse Code spin a set of ’70s and ’80s jazz fusion and R&B. The restaurant is busy and buzzing, there’s a nice crowd up front in the good speaker seats, at the top of the triangle. Some of the folks up front are quieting down to hear the music, even as more people stream in, even as the new arrivals are striking their poses, even as we realize that Anderson.Paak is at the bar, that Malibu cult singer-songwriter Ned Doheny is at a table in front of the DJ booth, that Kate Pierson is in the restaurant.

Darling is heating up, and the flavors, the sound, and the rhythm are continuing to evolve. The chef and the city are both learning to listen.

#6809. 12 12 12 Shakes Up the Tired Heist Formula with One Great Idea

Can the Anthony Mackie-led crime drama pull it off?

“A high-asset robbery, professionally conceived, is twelve months in planning, twelve hours in execution,” says Anthony Mackie’s character, FBI special agent Jack Strickland, at the start of the new Apple TV crime drama, 12 12 12. “A bank robber who is not caught within twelve days is not caught at all.”

It’s an amazing premise—honestly, one of the best I’ve heard in a long time. The first episode takes the viewer one month into planning the heist, one day into pulling off the heist, and one hour into their attempted escape. Then, episode 2 will move along to two months, two days, and two hours. Neat, right? But the big question for cocreators Jim Keeble and Dudi Appleton’s time-shifting heist series remains the same as any seemingly impossible bank job: Do they have the right team to pull it off?

From the two-episode premiere—now available to stream on Apple TV—I’d say we’re off to a very strong start. 12 12 12’s opening scene pays homage to Ocean’s 11’s Shaobo Qin when a contortionist unfolds his body and climbs out of a large safety deposit box. It’s one of the most genius ideas I’ve seen in a heist story of the last few years—I mean, talk about a man on the inside—especially in an overcrowded field of complex heist series. Remember Netflix’s Kaleidoscope, which allowed viewers to watch the story starting on any episode in the series? Well, 12 12 12 is far more than just a gimmick.

The story follows FBI special agent Jack Strickland (Mackie), who has tracked down one of the world’s best bank robbers, Paul Galvin (Jamie Dornan), to Zurich, Switzerland. Galvin and his unruly brother Bobby (Jack Kesy, who was last seen playing Robbie Pedergrast’s late brother on Task), made an enemy of Strickland when the FBI agent’s partner was killed as she attempted to arrest them during a major heist in Newark, New Jersey.

Now, Strickland has a personal vendetta against the man. He throws his philosophy that you’ll never catch a bank robber after twelve days of the robbery out the window—which is fine because the creators fabricated the whole “12 12 12” FBI rule anyway just to provide episodic framing for the series. (Maybe it resets now that Galvin is planning another heist?) Either way, 12 12 12 does a great job of making sure you never get lost in the three timelines.

Strickland’s white whale is also far from a one-note character himself. He’s a man following in his father’s footsteps to get out of a life of poverty, which would be easier if his brother Bobby wasn’t such of a wild card who can’t be trusted unattended for a single second.

As it turns out, Bobby gambled away all their money from the Newark heist, including Paul’s half. So his brother’s debtors force Paul back into a life of crime under threat of violence to his new lady love in France, his father-in-law, and their unborn child. It’s also quickly revealed that our contortionist is just one of the interesting members of Paul’s team, who are all tasked by Bobby’s mysterious benefactors with robbing a Zurich bank of 800,000 Swiss francs all owned by the Russian mafia.

From the first two episodes released so far, the 12 12 12 team seems like it has the right men for the job. The timeline-shifting format allows the series to do much more than simply break the action to provide a little backstory. As we move from months ahead to days after, and vice-versa, we also switch perspectives that allow for Paul and Jack to trade who is the hero and who is the villain.

Jack can be both the righteous FBI agent pursuing the bank robbers in the present and a man clearly far too hellbent on revenge in the past. The same goes for Paul, whose team blackmails, honeypots, and occasionally murders throughout the heist, while his backstory paints him as a good man who was forced down a dark road.

Where 12 12 12 falls short, at least so far, is in the script. Kari Skogland (who previously worked with Mackie on The Falcon and the Winter Soldier) directs the two-part premiere handily enough, but Keeble and Appleton’s writing is very formulaic. Scripts don’t always matter when you’re working with amazing characters like the contortionist lockpick. True Detective: Night Country’s Kali Reis is also on the team. Her mission is to hack into the subway system so that they can make their escape on public transit, which, of course, goes wrong. But when you have so much dialogue doubling as exposition in between the action, it feels like everyone’s conversations are more for the viewer than they are for each other.

For now, I’m hopeful that all the kinks are worked out once we move past the who, what, and why of 12 12 12’s opening episodes. Given the gimmick, and the fact that the series is only eight episodes instead of twelve, I’m excited to see just what throws the entire concept of the series into chaos.

#6808. What Does Transgender Mean? Everything You Need to Know, According to Experts

From gender identity to transition, here's what the term actually means.

The word "transgender" gets used a lot these days, but that doesn't necessarily indicate that everyone understands what being transgender actually means. Between headlines, social media debates, and ongoing conversations about gender identity, it's easy to feel like you're missing a piece of the puzzle or even worry about using the wrong terminology altogether. Fortunately, understanding what it means to be transgender doesn't have to be as complicated as the internet sometimes makes it seem.

"Being transgender means your gender identity doesn't align with the one you were assigned at birth. That's the whole heart of it," says licensed marriage and family therapist Adrienne Michelle, LMFT. "It doesn't tell you anything about how someone looks, what they've explored medically, or how far along they are in any journey. It's simply about your authentic sense of self being different from what you were told you were at the start."

Ultimately, gender is deeply personal, and there's no one "right" way to be transgender. Whether you're talking with a friend, supporting a loved one, or simply trying to better understand the world around you, having a basic understanding of transgender identity can make the conversation feel a whole lot less intimidating and a lot more inclusive.

The term "transgender" is an identity label that refers to people whose gender identity doesn’t match the sex they were assigned when they were born, explains therapist and LGBTQ+ educator Zoe Stoller. You'll also often hear transgender shortened to simply trans. The prefix trans- means "across" or "on the other side of," reflecting that a person's gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth.

"Gender is expansive, and 'trans' is the umbrella term that often holds all of that expansiveness," says Michelle. "It isn't a single identity; it's a beautifully wide category that makes room for so many people."

The transgender umbrella includes trans men, trans women, and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. And that's completely okay. The right label is the one that feels right to the person using it.

Although they're often used interchangeably, sex and gender aren't the same thing. Sex refers to the biological characteristics someone is labeled or assigned at birth, while gender refers to a person's internal sense of who they are. You may also come across the terms AFAB/AMAB (assigned female at birth/assigned male at birth), which describe the sex someone was assigned when they were born, not their gender identity. And while babies are often labeled male or female at birth, sex itself isn't always binary, as intersex people exist too.

Once you understand that distinction, a lot of the terminology starts to make more sense. Terms like nonbinary, genderfluid, genderqueer, and gender nonconforming all describe distinct experiences or expressions of gender, but they aren't interchangeable, and they don't all automatically mean someone is transgender. (P.S. If you're looking for a deeper breakdown of those identities, check out our complete gender identity glossary.)

"Gender nonconforming is about not feeling aligned with the normative, performative ways gender is 'supposed' to show up—the expectations around how a man or woman should look, dress, or move through the world," says Michelle. "It's about expression, not identity. You can be gender nonconforming and cisgender, and you can be gender nonconforming and trans. The two sometimes overlap, but they're answering different questions: transgender is about who you are, gender nonconforming is about how you show up."

As Stoller explains, the transgender umbrella includes both binary identities (such as trans men and trans women) and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. Like every other aspect of identity, everyone's relationship with gender is unique.

First things first: Despite the headline right above this, there's no one way to "look" transgender.

As Stoller explains, being transgender isn't defined by surgeries, medical procedures, or any other external milestone. The only thing someone needs to "count" as transgender is feeling that the label accurately reflects who they are. For many people, that identity is deeply affirming regardless of whether they ever choose to transition.

Transitioning simply refers to the process of aligning someone's life more closely with their gender identity. "It’s important to note that transition isn't a checklist," says Michelle. "Some people explore hormones or surgery, some focus on legal or social changes, some pursue none of it, and every one of those paths is whole and valid."

Transitioning can look different for everyone, but it's generally broken into a few categories:

Some people choose to align their outward appearance with their gender identity because it's an affirming—and sometimes life-saving—way to feel seen as their authentic selves, Stoller says. Others don't transition at all or may be unable to do so for personal, financial, or safety reasons. Regardless, their gender identity is no less valid.

Now that you know what transgender means, it's worth talking about how to actually use the term. According to Michelle, "transgender" is an adjective, not a noun. "You'd say 'a transgender person' or 'transgender people,' never 'a transgender' or 'the transgenders,'" she explains. "It works just like any other descriptive word; you wouldn't call someone 'a tall,' so the same care applies here."

As Stoller explains, it's also important not to assume someone identifies as transgender unless they've shared that information with you. Even if you think someone's gender identity technically falls under the transgender umbrella, it's ultimately up to them to decide which labels feel the most accurate and affirming. When in doubt, use the language someone uses to describe themselves.

One of the most common terminology mix-ups is the difference between a trans man and a trans woman. "A trans man is someone who was assigned female at birth and now identifies and lives as a man. A trans woman was assigned male at birth and identifies and lives as a woman," explains Michelle.

It's an important distinction because these terms describe who someone is, not who they used to be. In other words, trans men are men, and trans women are women. The word trans simply provides additional context about their gender journey—it doesn't change their gender.

As Stoller notes, some trans men and trans women identify fully within the traditional gender binary, while others may also feel connected to nonbinary experiences. Like every other aspect of gender identity, there's no one "right" way to experience or describe being transgender.

The short answer? Use the pronouns someone asks you to use. It's really that simple.

As Stoller explains, people—whether they're transgender or cisgender—can use whatever pronouns feel the most affirming to them, including multiple sets of pronouns or neopronouns. Although people often associate certain pronouns with certain genders, pronouns don't always tell you someone's gender identity.

If you aren't sure which pronouns someone uses, it's okay to politely ask. And if you accidentally get it wrong, apologize, correct yourself, and move on.

Another common misconception is that being transgender somehow determines who someone is attracted to. It doesn't.

Gender identity and sexual orientation are two completely different things (and both are different from romantic orientation), Michelle explains. Gender identity describes who someone is, while sexual orientation describes who they're attracted to. That means transgender people can be straight, gay, lesbian, bisexual, pansexual, asexual, or identify with any other sexual orientation—just like cisgender people.

Like gender identity, sexuality is deeply personal. Labels can be helpful, Stoller says, but they don't always capture the full picture of someone's experience, so it's always best to use the language someone chooses for themselves.

Being a good LGBTQ ally doesn't require having all the answers. It starts with listening, staying open to learning, and treating transgender people with the same respect you'd want someone to show you.

Use the name and pronouns someone asks you to use, and don't make assumptions based on their appearance or gender identity. As Michelle explains, a trans man might use he/him pronouns, a trans woman might use she/her, and a nonbinary person might use they/them—or another set of pronouns that feels most affirming to them. If you aren't sure, it's okay to politely ask.

You may also come across the word transsexual, particularly in older books, articles, or medical literature. While some transgender people have reclaimed the term for themselves, Stoller notes that it's generally considered more outdated and medicalized than transgender, and many people find it offensive. Unless someone has specifically told you that's the label they use, it's best to avoid it.

Above all, remember that every transgender person's experience is different. Listening, respecting the language people use to describe themselves, and remaining open to learning will go a long way.

If you've made it this far because you're wondering whether the word transgender might describe you, know that you don't have to have everything figured out today. Gender identity isn't a quiz you pass or fail, and there's no deadline for understanding yourself.

As Stoller explains, there's no single way to experience being transgender because every person's relationship with gender is different. Some people transition socially, medically, or legally, while others never transition at all. None of those experiences make someone's identity any more or less real.

If you'd like support, consider talking with an LGBTQIA+-affirming therapist, connecting with trusted friends or loved ones, or exploring resources from organizations like The Trevor Project can be a good place to start. You don't have to navigate questions about your identity alone.

As Michelle puts it, "Gender is expansive, full stop." The most important thing isn't fitting neatly into someone else's definition; it's giving yourself the space to discover what feels authentic to you.

#6798. MobLand Episode 9 Sets Up a Seriously Violent Finale

In season 2’s penultimate episode, Harry faces down Frankie to save Gina, while Kat McAllister calls Black Flag on the Harrigans.

Consider Tom Hardy’s smile. I know you’ve got a lot to think about right now, like the rising cost of a flat white, celebrity sex criminals, and which brand of pasta to stockpile, but put that on ice for a minute and consider Tom Hardy’s smile.

I could count on one hand the number of times I’ve seen him crack a grin on screen. He’s like those actors who’ll go nude only if it’s tasteful and in the name of art. Hardy’s smiles are anything but tasteful. When they happen, it’s as surprising as any jump scare.

Take episode 9 of MobLand, when Harry gloriously threatens Frankie. “That fucking face of yours, I’ll ‘ave that,” he growls like Burglar Bill’s psychopath cousin. “I’ll wear it to carnival as a mask when I cut it off with this pint glass.”

The eyebrows arch and the lips creak upward and the face cracks like a dam with a hairline fracture, exploding into a horrifying clown grin. Then… suddenly it’s gone, like a split atom, back into the cosmic darkness from whence it came. They say the best actors can tell a story with their eyes. Hardy can tell one with just his teeth, and it’s brilliant and it’s terrifying.

Anyway, that was my highlight of episode 9.

What else? Well, the cold open, where Frankie gatecrashes Kat’s ambassadors’ convention disguised as a waiter to threaten her. Kat is ready: “Under the 1961 Vienna Convention, unlawful entry into a sovereign embassy is not seen as trespass but as an attack on the nation. I call Black Flag and you do not make it off this fucking rug.”

Talking of smiles, Gina wipes one clean off Maeve’s face with an exit straight out of RuPaul’s Drag Race. You’ll recall Maeve’s dastardly scheme: persuade Gina to fake a suicide attempt, thus breaking Harry’s spirit and forcing him out. Instead, Gina sashays away, leaving a message in lipstick on the mirror: “Suck my balls, witch.”

Meow. I don’t think Ru could have put it better herself.

Maeve turns back to Eddie. “You want to be a Harrigan, there’s one last task: Harry.”

It’s not long before the smile returns to Maeve’s face, when she wakes to find Conrad in his pants, strapping on a bulletproof vest. “What are you doing, man? It’s not Friday. Friday night is bondage night!”

Actually, Conrad has pried a tasty piece of intel from JoJo, the Frankie henchman he tortured to death: a large shipment of drugs is landing at Tilbury Docks, ripe for the nicking. Off they drive with a boot full of weapons, soundtracked not by a true-crime podcast but by the bloodcurdling recording of JoJo’s torture.

Trouble is, just as they arrive, the ghouls of Conrad’s past start stalking the corridors of his mind again, whispering. “Leave me alone,” he cries, swerving the car. Maeve shows an almost uncharacteristic amount of empathy. “It’s okay,” she soothes.

Still, the heist is off. Conrad is in no fit state to add to the ranks of the ghosts he’s made. They drive home.

If there were an award for the weirdest scene of the series, it would go to Eddie asking Paul on a date. “Wondered if you fancied popping out for a pint later.” He might as well have asked him to go dogging in a Chipping Norton lay-by.

Really, he wants a bomb to kill Harry. Paul laughs it off with a monologue on the history of bombmaking, until Eddie plays his ace: “What if I tell you I know it was you who told Harry that I was in Ireland? I wonder what Maeve would think.” Paul’s smile dissolves. Maeve would have his guts for sausages. Or, in Eddie’s words: “How does it feel to have your bollocks in my briefcase?”

And so to the meat and potatoes of the episode: Harry and Frankie’s tête-à-tête.

It starts at Harry’s home. Jan has returned to him, having realized she’s safer in his giant hands than in anyone else’s. He wants to whisk her to a safehouse, urgently. So she does what anyone would do when their life is in imminent danger: run a bath, throw on some Damien Rice and soak her troubles away.

Trouble is, those troubles are literally downstairs with assault rifles, with Frankie close behind. Naturally, Harry keeps an even bigger gun under the sofa, so he festoons the walls of his home with their brains before leading Jan out to the car. Gina has just arrived. So, alas, has Frankie.

Still in his dressing gown, Frankie kidnaps Gina and spirits her off to the panic room/prison cell beneath his London home.

There, Gina and Frankie bond over a spot of smoothie play, hurling their drinks in each other’s faces, followed by a round of Nerf. It’s sweet. He likes her. Maybe he even gets her. She is, as he later tells Harry, “spunky, full of spunk.” That may just be what keeps her alive.

Over at Kevin HQ (a shipping container on an industrial estate), Harry, Jan and Kevin await news of Gina. Kevin pitches a new plan: go John Wick on the lot of them.

“You call Frankie and tell him we accept on the condition he returns Gina safely… then we meet Conrad and Maeve and blow their fucking brains out.” Then Eddie, then Frankie, then, through Bella’s nous, defenestrate Kat McAllister via legal channels.

Conrad, whose marbles have been rolling under the sofa all episode, is not expected to put up much of a fight.

“I’m taking over tonight,” fumes Kevin. “You’re either with me or against me.”

Well, no need for that just yet. Harry has a plan to save Gina. Leverage? Another child he can senselessly murder? No, no. Simply the good old-fashioned threat of violence.

At the parley, they’re yin and yang: Frankie the exuberant chatterbox, Harry the man who’ll never use two words when one will do. Frankie squeezes Harry’s arm: “Cor, feel those muscles. I mean, I’m tasty, but if we got in an actual ruck, I wouldn’t stand a snowflake. I bet you could kill me with your bare hands…”

“You’re not gonna kill her,” he growls wearily.

“You mind testing that?” And Frankie orders a goon to blow Gina’s head off with a shotgun and film it so they can watch.

He hands Harry the phone for the tersest of chats with his gaffer-taped daughter: “Try to stay calm. I’m gonna get you out of this as soon as I can.” Click. Frankie can’t believe it. “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

Harry’s reply is considered and simple: “If I was you, I would’ve had some really big, scary fucking geezers in here with you to keep you safe, to prevent the exact scenario that is unfolding right here.”

“B-b-b-but I’ve just got to pick up the phone,” stammers Frankie.

The tension is suddenly tighter than a celebrity sex criminal’s grip on the prison soap.

Harry’s purring now: “It took me seven seconds to walk through those doors from the car… No more cutting around in the old burgundy tracksuit, that’s for sure. Not without wearing a fucking colostomy bag hanging off your limp arse.”

Then the scary smile, the face-mask line and the clincher: “Speaking of seven seconds, that’s all you’ve got left to call your mate Joey and let Gina go. Three... two...”

But Frankie has one more trick up his tracksuit: he gets Kat McAllister on the phone. Remember the black flag stuff at the top of the episode?

She’s offering Harry another chance: “As of tonight there is no Harrigans. But the good news: you have a new job. Thank you for what you’re about to do for us. I am looking forward to you working for the team. Black Flag, motherfucker.”

In other words, Black Flag has been called, and the Harrigans are its target. It’s a fitting phrase. Pirates flew a black flag to tell ships to surrender or else. In motor racing, it means you’re disqualified. And it’s a snarling late-seventies hardcore punk band, whose “Nervous Breakdown” gives this episode its title. Kat’s version is all three at once.

Cut to the Cotswolds, where police are dredging the lake. They’ve pulled out a body. Is it Richie’s? It’s someone’s, and I’d wager they didn’t die smiling. Hardy, on the other hand, lives to smile another day.

The chips are down, the traps are laid, and there’s one episode left. Does Frankie have one last plan up his tracksuit? Will Conrad pull his brain back from the brink? Will Maeve get the last laugh? Or will it be Harry, God help us all?

As Shakespeare puts it in Macbeth: “There’s daggers in men’s smiles: the near in blood, the nearer bloody.”
