#6797. Design Experts Reveal When It’s Actually OK to Start Decorating for the Holidays

There’s no hard rule—but your HOA may have one.

Now that the Halloween candy bowls are empty, you might find thoughts of tinsel, garland, and fairy lights dancing in your head, but is it really time to trade your pumpkins for holiday trimmings? Some have strong opinions on the topic, half believing the jolly spirit of the holiday season doesn't start until after Thanksgiving, the other rushing to unbox their silver bells as soon as the clock strikes 12 on November 1—but the choice isn’t always yours to make.

If you belong to a Homeowners’ Association (and over 78.1 million Americans do), the question of when to put up your outdoor décor may be dictated by their guidelines. HOAs tend to have rules about holiday decorations, restrictions covering their location (within the boundaries of your yard, of course), sound level (not too loud), safety (no open flames), light color, and when the decorations can be installed. In some communities, you can even be fined for bringing out the twinkle lights and nativity creche before Thanksgiving—this happened to one Florida man in 2002, when he was hit with a fine of $1,000 for putting his lights up on November 6.

If you don’t live in an HOA, you may not get fined for decorating too early, but you will get judged by neighbors who find pre-Thanksgiving holiday displays a little, well, tacky. To be fair, holding until after Thanksgiving does have its perks—why would you pass on the opportunity to decorate a gorgeous fall tablescape? In the spirit of peace on earth and goodwill toward man, we asked design experts what to do. Here’s what they said.

Additional copy by Meghan Shouse and Alyssa Khan.

You might follow a religious tradition that guides your holiday decorating, like placing the Baby Jesus in the nativity on Christmas Day or keeping the tree up until Epiphany. But when it comes to secular etiquette, there are no hard-and-fast rules about when to start (or stop) decking the halls.

“Most people find it tasteful to stick within the weeks or the month of the holiday itself,” points out Lizzie Post, co-president of The Emily Post Institute and author of Emily Post’s Etiquette—The Centennial Edition. “That said, there is no etiquette that dictates it. It’s the same way you can paint your house neon orange with blue stripes—there’s nothing that says you can’t. It’s really, do you want to be the person who does?”

For designer Tan France, the answer to that question is decidedly yes. For the past decade, he and his husband have been decorating their tree on Halloween and taking it down on Valentine’s Day. “I’m British, and we don’t really do Halloween,” he explains. “When I moved here 16 years ago, I was more obsessed with Christmas. So on Halloween day, when everyone else is crafting their costumes, I want to be decorating the tree. And a tree brings my husband and me a lot of joy when we come down and turn the lights on first thing in the morning. It’s just a lovely thing to perk your day up.”

Do people question France about his decision to put up decorations so early, and for so long? “Oh gosh, yeah, everyone asks that,” he says. “Online, the press, neighbors. And my answer is really simple: Why delay joy?”

If you are jonesing to do some holiday decorating, there's good news: While Christmas might be weeks away, there is one happening right now. It’s called Thanksgiving.

“Anytime before midnight of Thanksgiving is too early to decorate,” insists Wendy Kerrigan, founder of Atelier Design in New Orleans. “Filling the house with Christmas decorations too soon really takes away from the beauty of Thanksgiving, moving your mind into the bustle of Christmas.”

Kerrigan wishes more people would celebrate the fall season for longer; rushing to the tinsel takes away from the beauty of the autumn season, “Thanksgiving can be experienced in a special way too, filling the house with fall colors, fall leaves, gourds, and cornucopia,” says Kerrigan. “Instead of trying to hurry the year along, enjoy each family celebration for what it is.”

Even more, sprinting to the Yuletide season robs the holiday of its own magic. “What makes things special when they’re once a year is that they’re experiences you look forward to,” says Vermont-based interior designer Joshua Smith. “How many holidays can we take at one time? Are we doing Black Friday in July now? Let’s just enjoy the season that we’re in!”

When it comes to holiday decorating, many hands make light work. Several designers say they take advantage of the long holiday weekend and enforced family time to rope in some extra help making spirits bright. “In my parents' home, we start decorating right after Thanksgiving dinner. It’s a rare opportunity for everyone to be together, and my siblings and I stay to help out our parents,” says New York-based designer Mikel Welch. “Plus, it’s a much better tradition than terrorizing sales associates on Black Friday Eve!"

Smith has a plan for bringing the Christmas spirit to his home, without shouldering the burden of doing all the hall-decking himself. Last year, his family decorated their trees over Thanksgiving weekend and made it a tree-decorating contest. With his husband's family in town, “they’ll be divided into three teams, decorating three trees, and I’ll go around at the end judging, and everyone will win prizes. We’re not all going to be together at Christmas, so this activity is a fun way to celebrate the season and not do all the work myself,” he says.

Still, early decorators would argue that the amount of effort needed to set up holiday decor is another reason to put up decorations early—and keep them up late. “This is the most miserable time of the year; it gets dark so early, it’s so darn cold. I just think you should do everything you can to bring yourself joy,” says France. “Why put in all that effort for a few weeks?”

Even those who are normally on the side of waiting to decorate agree that desperate times call for dopamine decorating. Whether it’s the state of your inbox or the state of the world that is stressing you out, if you’re feeling a little blue, go right ahead and pull out the red and green.

“In a perfect calendar, the day after Thanksgiving would be my official start to the holiday season—it's traditionally the day when my family goes out into the woods of Tahoe National Forest to chop down our tree, drag it home, and decorate it,” says designer Noz Nozawa of Noz Design. “But this year, the day after Halloween is when I let the holidays start—there's plenty to worry about, and having my nutcrackers and string lights out a few weeks early can only boost spirits.”

Whenever you decorate, it can be a way to bring fun to a not-always joyful world. “We have no control over a lot of things in our lives, but when we walk through the door of our home, we choose to focus our energy and attention on whether that is on fear or on love and joy,” says Smith. “And this holiday season, I think more than ever we have to focus on ways to bring more light to ourselves and to each other.”

When it comes to early decor, “I can handle it from my neighbor; I cannot handle it from a corporation,” says Post. That may be because we know the Christmas displays popping up in a big-box store alongside the Halloween candy are trying to sell us something, while a neighbor decorating is spreading joy, spending their own time and money to brighten the dark winter nights. For a shop, holiday decorating is mercenary. For an individual, it’s a mitzvah.

If you appreciate the joy inspired by holiday displays and do want to start setting them up early or keep them up late, consider finding a middle ground by leaning toward decor that’s more about lighting up the night, rather than marking a particular occasion. “I see a lot of people in Vermont leaving the actual lights up, not so much like blow-up Santas or things like that, but the actual lights up for the entire winter,” says Post.

If you’re a minimalist (or live in a studio apartment), you don’t need a tree to decorate. “If you don’t have a tree, I’m going to invite you to place a hurricane lamp on your coffee table with a wreath of greenery around it and light a candle in the evening,” says Smith. “Because what we know from neuroscience and neuroaesthetics is, you’ve got to engage the senses to nourish the soul!” Light a candle or turn on some twinkle lights, and you’re liable to spark joy as well.

France would argue that even his trees aren’t occasion-specific. “Here’s the thing: I’m Muslim, and my tree and the lights on our house aren’t necessarily for Christmas,” he explains. “It’s to celebrate Thanksgiving, Christmas, and the New Year. This is the most miserable time of the year. It gets dark so early, it gets light so late, it’s so darn cold. I just think do everything you can to bring yourself joy.”

Though you’d never find them rushing to hang their stockings, the designer duo behind the Kansas City-based firm Kobel + Co, Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, say there’s one design scheme you can always tap into if you find yourself in the class of outliers running to get decor up before Christmas Eve. “Ralph Lauren Holiday isn't just having a moment; it's a classic holiday go-to for so many reasons,” Bennett explains. “Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette. Layer in antique silver pieces and simple glass ornaments. A little patina and variation keep everything from feeling too matched. The goal is a warm, welcoming home with pieces that feel collected and personal.” After all, you want it to feel like home, even at Christmas.

#6793. These 9 Best Waxed Canvas Jackets Are Good-Looking and Hard-Wearing

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Nothing is better suited for cold, wet weather.

Why did we ever stray from this path? A good waxed canvas jacket will handle rough weather, sure—any new-age rain jacket can do that as well. But the magic of this cloth is the look. Made from heavy-duty cotton canvas treated with an oil-based waxed coating, these jackets offer a barrier against rain and snow while also blocking the wind—but they look damn good doing it.

Once you have a waxed canvas jacket, it becomes uniquely yours, developing a weathered patina with every wear. And if you give it a fresh coat of wax every year or so, these jackets will stay waterproof and last for most, if not all, of your life.

Here, we’ve rounded up our favorite styles from the names you’d expect, like Barbour, Filson, and L.L. Bean, alongside a few brands you might not know yet. Ready to brave the unexpected fall and winter weather? With one of these jackets, you will be.

An enduring icon thanks to its tenacious popularity, the Barbour Beaufort waxed jacket boasts all the classic details created by Dame Margaret Barbour, including a color-contrast corduroy collar (which you can turn up and secure when it gets cold), paneled tartan lining, flapped bellow pockets, and a two-way front zip. It’s truly an evergreen piece that will never go out of style, and Style Director Jonathan Evans argues that if you’re going to invest in a waxed canvas jacket, it should be the Beaufort.

For a chic, elevated look (pretty hard to achieve in rainy weather), Tom Beckbe’s Tensaw is a great pick. The Alabama-based brand has that hunting tradition, but it doesn’t go full upland. It’s more elevated than that.

Since 1948, the Trialmaster jacket has been Belstaff's signature style, its origin going back to the Scottish Six Days Trial, a motorcycle competition that attracts riders worldwide. Developed to keep the competitors dry in tough weather conditions, it’s built with black waxed cotton and features a legacy slanted map pocket and an adjustable belt, so you can always find the perfect fit.

Made from Filson’s signature waxed cotton, this jacket has protected foresters and outdoorsmen from brambles and abrasion in the rain-soaked forests of the Pacific Northwest since the 1920s—talk about an endorsement. The hip-length cut allows for easy access to pockets or a tool belt, and this type of fit will also stop the jacket from riding up when you’re seated.

For a super-warm option, Flint and Tinder’s flannel-lined style ensures you won’t feel a shiver or a sprinkle. Tough, timeless, and made in the U.S.A., it’s constructed with a weather-resistant Martexin seven-ounce sailcloth, which, like selvage denim or finely tanned leather, will only get better with age. It’s more lightweight than denim or leather, but still substantial enough to feel durable. Take it from our endorsement of the jacket:

“It’s not even like a techy Arc’teryx shell you might want to be more careful with because of the weight. This is the kind of jacket you don’t think twice about wearing. You wear it in the rain, not worrying if it brushes against brick walls. You toss it in the backseat of your car—or, even better, the backseat of a side-by-side on a weekend in the woods. Over time—years, I mean—the fabric picks up creases. They don’t ruin it. They define it. It becomes yours.”

The first thing you’ll notice about this jacket is that it’s actually true to size and has a great fit. It leaves just enough room for layering without the risk of looking sloppy, and it’s slim enough without feeling too restrictive. We also loved how comfortable it is and like that the weathered fabric gives it a classic vintage appeal (without having to spend hours searching a secondhand shop).

TBC doesn’t just make damn good boots—the brand has perfected the durable, rugged, highly functional jacket that doesn’t sacrifice style for comfort. We like how the water-resistant finish only gets better with age and how the polyester sleeve lining both allows for ease of movement and prevents the sleeves from bunching up. Plus, it’s great for colder days thanks to the warm blanket flannel lining.

Tecovas keeps it cool, casual, and authentic with a waxed canvas ranch jacket that makes an everyday outfit look even better than it would sans jacket. Whether you’re mending fence posts or making a quick dash to the office or pub, this jacket will help muzzle the nip in the air and keep downpours at bay. It’s lightweight and less bulky than some of the other options on this list, and it does have soft brushed lining on the inside for some added warmth. The Western-inspired details also set it apart from the more classic styles.

L.L. Bean, the brand known for its rugged outdoor gear, does a cool, modern twist on a classic hunting coat. Working with the originator of waxed cotton, British Millerain, the company designed this option with an innovative fabric that eliminates the typical cleaning fuss that comes with this type of garment.

#6792. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

Related Story: Almost Every Piece in This Layered Arkansas Home Was a Lucky Antique Find

Step through the citron entry and enter the round room, designed in collaboration by Helen Bergin Interiors and Lichten Architects. The walls are softened by long curtains from The Shade Store, which create beautiful juxtaposition with the curved, sculptural silver media console by Asher Gillman. The actual turntable is flush to the surface, giving it a seamless look while it emits wonderful sound.

In the Withdrawing Room by Jen Stevens of Fonde Interiors, plaster botanicals by artist Amanda Norman come to life as they crawl from the mantel along the chimney breast, escaping to the ceiling. The vines were handmade by Norman and capture the essence of a garden left undisturbed, showing that this formal-at-first-glance room has a wilder side.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Related Story: A Trip to the Lone Star State Inspired the Earthy Textures of This Tennessee New Build

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.

#6790. I Found the Most Comfortable Pointed-Toe Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Buying pointed-toe boots has always been a challenge for me because I’m built like a ballerina—not, sadly, in terms of long legs or elegant arms or a swanlike neck, but in terms of feet. I have the bunions of a woman who has dedicated her life to the art of dance, which is ironic because I took ballet lessons for all of three months when I was eight. (I quit after they let me wear the tutu, which was really the only thing I was in it for.) I am confessing this not because I enjoy talking about my feet on the internet, but to establish my credibility when I say that I have finally found a pair of slim, sexy, pointed-toe boots that are comfortable enough to wear all day, even for those of us with less-than-ideal extremities.

Margaux’s Inga boots have a long, squared-off toe and a little kitten heel that measures 1.5 inches—tall enough to give you a bit of a lift, but low enough to be walkable. They come in black leather and a sleek espresso suede, both of which look great with jeans but would also be cute peeking out of long, drapey trousers. The best part is the sizing: Margaux offers its boots in narrow, medium, and wide, and somehow, through the magic of proportion play, the wide does not look wide. They accommodate the fullest part of my foot without looking bulky or breaking up the nice long line.

The first time I wore them, it was to a witches’ dinner that my friend Mo throws every Halloween. She invites 30 of her best female friends over, decorates her home in high goth style, and serves us a theatrically witchy meal. (This time, it was black squid-ink pasta.) Of course, one has to dress appropriately to dine with the coven, so I paired the boots with slim black jeans and a sheer black polka-dotted top. Not only did I get a bunch of compliments, but I didn’t find myself pining for my broomstick on the walk home—the boots were just as comfortable at the end of the evening as they’d been at the start of the night.

They’ve held up equally well on the commute to work. In addition to the extra width, they also come with five millimeters of cushioning on the inside, which is enough to protect you when you’re pounding the pavement. Ever since slimmer toes reentered the fashion conversation, I’ve been faced with the choice between sitting out the trend or hobbling around in boots that fundamentally do not fit. As much as I love a good pair of shoes, I also live for a long walk, and the Ingas, miraculously, let me have both. Really, they don’t feel like heels at all, which is a rare and wonderful thing for a pair of pointed-toe kitten-heel boots.

#6789. We Tested Luxury Sheet Sets for Three Years—These Are the Only Ones Worth the Splurge

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

For hot sleepers, restless sleepers, and anyone tired of scratchy bedding.

You deserve better than itchy, scratchy sheets on your bed at night. Upgrading your bedding is more than a bedroom aesthetic choice; it’s an investment in your well-being. I know this firsthand—as both a design editor and an insomniac. I’ve tested over a dozen of the best luxury sheets on the market in my journey to fix my sleep routine. The right sheet set lets me drift off much more easily, and I’m here to help you find one of your own.

What sets “luxury” sheets apart from your run-of-the-mill bargain bedding? It's a combination of texture, quality, aesthetic, and price point. The set should feel good the moment it hits your skin. The fabric, too, should be durable and constructed to avoid pilling, tearing, or any of that less-than-best feel. As the name implies, luxury sheets often come with a higher price tag, so you'll want to make sure they're worth the extra money.

I have every sleep issue you could think of: I'm a hot sleeper, I snore, I toss and turn, and I have eczema, so I have to be picky about what fabric goes in my bed. Luckily, my fellow editors, testers, and I have spent over three years sleeping on every kind of sheet under the sun, from fluffy flannel to silky eucalyptus. And now we’re revealing the ones that totally upgraded our sleep routines. Scroll on to meet our MVBs (most valuable bedding).

I first tested the Buffy Breeze sheets in my evaluation, and beyond the beautiful sheen that made the sheets look luxe, the cooling feeling of the eucalyptus fibers completely won me over. After months of trying other sheets, these were still my favorite.

I'm an incredibly hot sleeper, and I can't stand waking up to sticky sheets. These sheets from Buffy, made from moisture-wicking eucalyptus, gave me the most refreshing sleep I've had in my life. The first quality that impressed me about these sheets was how well they shifted with my constant tossing and turning. Even as they gathered underneath me, I was never uncomfortable. They're lightweight and breathable without the gauzy texture of other airy sheets. I genuinely looked forward to sinking into them at the end of the day.

I also tested Buffy's Cloud pillows under the pillowcases in this set and was impressed with how well they held their shape, even as I bunched them up under my neck. I was convinced I’d have horrible neck pain any time I slept on a pillow that wasn’t the butterfly shape I have for side sleepers. Imagine how shocked I was by how comfortable and long I slept.

Colorways: 25 | Sizes: Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: Eucalyptus (lyocell)

Quince bedding is one of the best ways to get that luxury sheet feel on a tighter budget. Tester Jessica Cherner has tested their bamboo, linen, and percale varieties, but the bamboo sheets stood out for their silky feel. "These sheets are so great because they have a tiny bit of stretch to them, which makes them even softer," Cherner says. "What makes them so soft is the fact that they're made from 100 percent viscose bamboo (the pulp), which is a bit more luxurious than rayon."

Colorways: 15 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: 100 percent viscose from organic bamboo

Many die-hard fans of cotton bedding can’t imagine sleeping on anything else. The cool, crisp feeling of Hommey’s striped cotton bedding is what won over McKenzie. “These are my perfect all-purpose sheets,” McKenzie says. “It’s not for your hottest days or your coldest nights, but for all of the days that fall in between. It’s a sturdy, well-woven 100 percent organic cotton, designed for year-round use and reliable comfort. It has a crisp feel that maintains its shape, and it rarely wrinkles, even after I’ve spent all night tossing and turning. I’m also a hot sleeper, and any thread count above 400 TC starts to get a bit stuffy for my taste. These fall at a perfect 300 TC, thick enough to insulate from cool air.”

Colorways: 28 | Sizes: Twin/Twin XL, Full/Queen, King/Cal King | Materials: 100 percent organic cotton

Despite being a hot sleeper, McKenzie still needed a solid set of winter sheets for the coldest nights. The whipstitching on Boll & Branch’s flannel set made for a festive refresh in the holiday season. “I've also tested Boll & Branch's cashmere sheets, and those were more lightweight than the flannel, but they have the same brushed, soft weave,” McKenzie says. “It's as durable as my Saatva flannel sheets, but not as stiff. They look super chic, like hotel sheets. It takes a lot for me to willingly own light-colored sheets, but these are just gorgeous. My favorite detail is the holly-red whipstitching; I just wish it went all the way around the border of the set. I was sad to take these off my bed to test the others.”

Colorways: 15 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King w/ King Cases, King w/ Std. Cases, California King, Split King | Materials: 100 percent organic cotton

Piglet in Bed allows customers to choose between 100 percent linen and linen-blend sheets, in order to maximize personal comfort. Tester Maggie Haddad opted for the linen blend and was pleasantly surprised. “After sleeping on this bedding for more than two months, I can confidently say I don’t see myself going back to traditional cotton sheets anytime soon,” Haddad says. “I initially worried that linen might feel too coarse or scratchy for my liking, but the linen blend turned out to be the ideal balance of softness and subtle texture.”

Colorways: 13 | Sizes: Twin, Full, Queen, King | Materials: 100 percent linen or 55 percent European linen and 45 percent long staple cotton

Don’t be intimidated by cashmere’s delicate nature. Though the cleaning instructions are specific, these sheets are worth extra TLC. “Just saying ‘cashmere sheets’ has an air of luxury, and Boll and Branch's set lives up to the hype. From night one, these sheets draped over me like silk, but were more buttery soft and fuzzy than cold and smooth. As someone who sweats easily, the lightweight feel was welcome. I'd still reach for these sheets in the summer, or whenever I had a guest I wanted to impress.”

Colorways: 4 | Sizes: Queen, King with King Cases, Cal King | Materials: 92 percent extra-long-staple organic Turkish cotton, 8 percent cashmere

When contributing writer Kelsey Mulvey put these Luxome sheets to the test, every little detail wowed her, even as a sheets snob. "Unless I'm able to set the thermostat to an arctic temperature, I run hot when I sleep, so I'm always looking for bed sheets with cooling properties," Mulvey says. Not only is the viscose from bamboo used here moisture-wicking and temperature-regulating, so I'm not waking up in a pool of sweat, but they're also ridiculously silky smooth."

Colorways: 12 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King, Split King | Materials: 100 percent Bamboo viscose

Senior Commerce Editor Marina Liao notes that Parachute's linen sheets feel soft right out of the packaging and regulate one's body temperature better than other linen sheets. She uses these year-round. "I love that the sheets don't shed much, and I trust the brand to deliver consistent quality because of its transparency in providing details like GSM," Liao says. "The name evokes many positive associations in my mind, and this would be a nice luxury set I'd gift to someone moving into their first apartment."

Colorways: 8 | Sizes: Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: 100 percent European flax

Cherner would pay twice as much for these Lands' End sheets. "Here's why: I prioritize softness over all else, and these are definitely some of the softest sheets I've ever slept on," Cherner says. "They don't really have a hint of crispness, so if that's your thing, you may not love these. Plus, when I think about the other supremely soft sheets I have—most of which are more than double the price of these Lands' End ones—I feel like I'm getting a bargain."

Colorways: 8 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King, Split King | Materials: 100 percent Premium Supima cotton

I originally tested these sheets because they were designed for my new Purple mattress, not expecting much. After months of sleeping on them, they'll be my new favorite all-purpose sheets. They're soft and stay put, so making the bed in the morning is a breeze. And I could use them no matter the season—they strike that perfect balance of warming and cooling. It's flowy and always looks neat. Even though it's meant to optimize the comfort of a Purple mattress, I'm confident it would make any mattress brand feel softer.

Colorways: 6 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King, Split King | Materials: 63 percent rayon from bamboo viscose, 27 percent polyester, 10 percent spandex

As someone with sensitive skin, finding sheets that are gentle on my skin is essential. I don't want something scratchy and itchy that'll cause my eczema to flare up, so please trust me when I say they're the softest sheets I've ever tested. It's made of 700 threads of long-staple Giza cotton, for durability and warmth. Giza cotton is a sought-after, typically expensive fabric, so it's quite the steal to get a set for under $200. I normally only sleep on silk pillowcases, but I make an exception for how smooth this fabric is.

Colorways: 8 | Sizes: Twin, Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: 100 percent Giza sateen cotton

Is there anything cooler than sheets so comfy, they're approved by NASA? Tester Rachel Elliot put the Slumber Cloud Performance Cotton sheets to the test. The sheets are made from a unique blend of cotton and viscose that received the Certified Space Technology quality mark. Translation? The same technology used to design specialized NASA spacesuits is used for these DTC bedsheets. It's engineered to strike that perfect balance between a cozy feel and temperature regulation. They'll cool you off when you're hot and warm you up when you're cold. "I tried these in peak New York City winter and actually did feel warmed up after about 30 minutes in bed," Elliot says. "Now that it's warm outside, I do have to admit that these are very crisp and cooling!"

Colorways: Four | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: 30 percent Outlast viscose, 70 percent cotton

Tester Laura Tarafa first tried Frette sheets on an Italy vacation in 2021, and she had some of the best sleep of her life. There's a reason five-star properties like the Le Sirenuse, Four Seasons Hotel Milano, and The St. Regis hotels use Frette linens. "Though the sheets were cotton, it felt like I was sleeping on a silk cloud, and I didn't slide around on the bed," Tarafa says. "I was genuinely excited to end the day of sightseeing in Italy in order to get back into bed and feel the comfort of the sheets again! In fact, I loved them so much that while I was still on my trip, I ordered a few items from Frette's Lux Percalle collection and their H by Frette range to my home in Texas."

There's a delicate balance between crisp and uncomfortable when it comes to percale bedding, and Brooklinen stays firmly on the right side. "Over the years, my percale sheets have remained crisp and cool to the touch, but never feel stiff or scratchy," Mulvey says. "In fact, I like this set so much that I bought a Twin version for the trundle bed in our guest room—you know, to make my friends and family feel like they’re in a hotel."

Tester Katelyn Lunders chose this set as her first sateen sheets, and loved the breathable feeling of the organic bamboo fabric. The hypoallergenic sheets are made with non-toxic solvents, so it's a great choice for sensitive skin. "I love how lightweight and cooling they are, and they don't wrinkle as easily as my cotton sets. The only con I have is that because the fabric is on the thinner side, it slightly shows the outline of my beloved Coop pillows, as you can see in the photo, but regardless, they are my current go-to."

#6785. Jensen Huang’s Shoes Were the Tell

I like men’s shoes and I’ve sold men’s shoes. A man’s footwear reveals facets of his public persona—and his private desires.

I spent much of Thursday staring at photographs of Jensen Huang’s shoes arranged in one soldierly line longer in both directions than the frame could hold. These dozen pairs—each different, all dazzling—were but a glimpse of his footwear armies. Jensen’s Hermès slip-ons and dozens of black leather jackets are not only his fashion signatures; they’re also proof of his decision to not waste precious energy deciding what clothes to wear each morning.

This is a philosophy I share with Jensen, and my version saves even more time and energy: Don’t change clothes. But I like men’s shoes, I’ve sold men’s shoes, and I believe that a man’s shoes reveal facets of his public persona and private desires.

In Jensen’s case, there’s no mystery. He’s a brilliant guy who was once a small, smart immigrant kid bullied and beaten at a Kentucky Baptist reform school that his uncle mistook for a boarding school. Now he’s 63, a five-foot-six AI titan standing in adorable—size 9 is my guess—multi-thousand-dollar slip-ons atop his $140 billion, with his dick out and dripping all over his kicks du jour.

And we are left to sort the tragedy, seek catharsis, and remind ourselves again—and again—that Jensen Huang’s downfall awaits any man who ignores the fundamental law of our own animal nature: Ven der putz shteht, der sechel licht in drert: When the penis stands, the brain goes to hell.

I make no moral judgment here; we have no basis or right to do so. The college sweetheart he married 41 years ago, along with their two grown children, are the only souls with standing in this case. But I think it’s only fair, in light of current reporting on Jensen’s three concurrent affairs—that’s two side pieces plus Princess Honey-Pot plus a wife—to reconsider that vast battalion of decision-saving slip-ons and what it says.

The shoes reveal a man with smallish feet whose penis was too busy making decisions to consider laces, love, or the welfare of his family and business. And there aren’t enough shoes, dollars, or women in the world to fill the hole in Jensen Huang.

The Latin word ‘invidia’ means envy. This, given Jensen’s shoes, is not coincidence or irony; it’s a weakling’s shamed confession. Hermes needs to cobble one last pair for Jensen Huang, long, bright red, and floppy.

#6784. The 5 Best Skin Care Tools for Women Over 50 That Are Worth the Investment, According to Experts

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Give your beauty routine an upgrade with these transformative tools.

There’s no shortage of skin care tools on the market. And if you’ve ever found yourself clicking through page after page of LED masks, microcurrent devices, and sculpting gadgets, wondering which ones are actually worth the investment, you’re not alone. With so many options to choose from, I set out to find the tools best suited for mature skin, focusing on the technologies that can complement a good routine rather than overwhelm it.

After 50, skin naturally begins to change. Collagen production slows, cellular turnover decreases, and hormonal shifts can bring new concerns like dryness, sensitivity, and changes in elasticity and pigmentation. But that doesn’t mean your routine needs to get more aggressive. In fact, the experts I spoke with emphasized quite the opposite: Mature skin benefits from thoughtful stimulation, consistency, and a healthy skin barrier—not from piling on increasingly intense treatments.

“Skin doesn’t need to be punished to improve,” says celebrity master esthetician Melissa Fox. Board-certified aesthetic physician associate at Row 89, Rocio Romero, agrees, pointing out that the best device is ultimately the one you’ll actually use consistently. After all, an expensive gadget collecting dust in your bathroom cabinet isn’t doing anything for your complexion.

After consulting experts, researching the options, and testing a few myself, I narrowed the crowded field down to five tools that stood out for their ability to support mature skin. Below, shop the best devices to improve firmness and radiance while temporarily reducing puffiness and sculpting the face.

“Red light is my number one,” says Dr. Romero. The wavelengths help regenerate cells and reduce inflammation. “There are different versions, and the trick is to pick the one that fits your lifestyle, because you have to be consistent with it,” she adds. This Celluma device is amazing because it has three modes (anti-aging, acne, and pain relief), and its large panel design works on your face and across your body.

Expert review: “Celluma is one of my favorite technologies because it’s gentle, noninvasive, and easy to incorporate into a consistent routine. Red and near-infrared light can help support collagen production and improve the appearance of fine lines over time. I especially love LED for mature skin because you’re encouraging healthier-looking skin without creating unnecessary inflammation. It’s a perfect example of progressive, not aggressive.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

Microcurrent devices like Nuface’s Trinity+ work wonders for lifting, tightening, and sculpting. Dr. Rocio recommends using the device on your face, neck, chest, and under-eyes. “Especially if you have Botox or filler, there are specific areas where it works beautifully,” she adds. “The contraction of the muscle will metabolize your injectables faster.”

Expert review: “Microcurrent is one of my favorite categories for women who want to address the appearance of facial contours. It uses a very low-level electrical current, and some people notice a temporary improvement in facial definition and tone. I particularly like it for clients focused on the jawline and cheek area.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

“Lyma is a great option for women who are serious about investing in advanced at-home skin care technology,” says Dr. Melissa Fox. It uses low-level laser light rather than the heat-based resurfacing approach associated with many professional lasers. It’s particularly helpful for those concerned about fine lines, texture, and overall skin quality.

Best for: Improving skin tone and elasticity

Customer review: “If you’re willing to put in the work, this will work for you! It is one of the few products that has actually changed my skin. It does take commitment, but it’s better than going somewhere for a pricey treatment. I just do it at home while watching TV!”

“Another tool that is so simple and you have at home already is ice,” says Dr. Romero. Dr. Fox concurs, saying, “Anyone who knows me knows I’m obsessed with cold therapy. I tell my clients to ice their skin daily, and cryo sticks are one of the easiest ways to incorporate cooling into a routine.” Cryo globes are the easy, luxe, and mess-free way to enjoy the process.

Expert review: “They’re wonderful for temporarily reducing puffiness, soothing the skin, and making the face look refreshed and more sculpted. I especially love them in the morning around the jawline, cheeks, and under-eye area. They don’t replace collagen-building treatments, but the immediate cosmetic benefits are beautiful.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

“Gua sha brushes are such an underrated tool,” says Dr. Fox. “Gua sha is my go-to,” Dr. Romero agrees. Gentle, deliberate movements can help temporarily reduce facial puffiness and relax areas where you hold tension, particularly around the jaw and brow.

Expert review: “As we age, the face can look more tired simply because of fluid retention or muscle tension. Gua sha is a wonderful way to bring movement and relaxation into a routine. It’s also a beautiful self-care ritual, although it won’t permanently lift the skin or replace collagen.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

Melissa Fox, celebrity master esthetician and the founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach.

Frequency depends entirely on the technology. LED can often be used several times per week, or even daily when the device’s protocol allows it. Cooling tools can be incorporated daily, and gua shas can be used a few times a week with very gentle pressure. Microcurrent should follow the specific device’s instructions, though most recommend a few times per week as well. “My biggest advice is not to use everything at once,” says Dr. Fox. “More isn’t necessarily better, especially when skin becomes more reactive with age.” Choose two or three tools that address your concerns, stay consistent, and prioritize barrier health. Skin longevity is a marathon, not a sprint.

As a leading publisher of fashion, lifestyle, and beauty content, ELLE.com is committed to highlighting the best products in various categories by personally testing the latest and most innovative products, interviewing countless experts, and vetting customer-loved items. For this piece, ELLE associate beauty commerce writer Emma Aerin Becker consulted board-certified dermatologists to learn all about the best skin care tools for women over 50. She also tested them out and combed through hundreds of customer reviews, assessing each one based on its features and customer evaluations.

#6778. Léa Seydoux Isn’t Trying to Act. She’s Trying to Live

The French actress continues her string of emotionally demanding roles in The Unknown and Gentle Monster

Léa Seydoux first stepped into the limelight in Christophe Honoré’s The Beautiful Person, where her role as Junie de Chartres earned her the Trophée Chopard award for rising actors at Cannes. From there, she moved seamlessly between highbrow French cinema and Hollywood, achieving a critical breakthrough with Blue Is the Warmest Color in 2013 and starring alongside Daniel Craig as a Bond girl in Spectre and No Time to Die. Most years, she’s starred in at least two films, and 2026 is no exception: She plays a woman with a man’s soul trapped in her body in Arthur Harari’s psychological fantasy The Unknown, and in Marie Kreutzer’s Gentle Monster, releasing on Netflix on November 18, she’s a pianist who unearths her husband’s hideous crimes. The words thorny and barbed read like understatements for both films, and it’s exactly what interests Seydoux about the stories. Like in life itself, she avoids easy answers, opting instead for exploration.

Seydoux got the chance to explore an especially ambitious idea in The Unknown. It tells the story of David Zimmerman (Niels Schneider), a photographer who wakes up in the body of a woman he sleeps with. Seydoux plays both that woman, Eva, and the man trapped within her body. “I felt the movie was something new to me, to play an impossible idea and make it believable, try to give it a truthful dimension,” Seydoux says. She’s no stranger to high-concept stories, previously starring in Bertrand Bonello’s The Beast and David Cronenberg’s Crimes of the Future. Each time, she imbues these characters with humanity, despite their far-fetched sci-fi dilemmas.

“I like movies that make you think, movies that bring up questions, when there’s a debate around the film,” she says. “What I love the most is to explore human nature, our human condition. This is what cinema allows us to do.”

Seydoux might vehemently love movies, yet acting remains a demanding process for her. She relies on the art form to dig into feelings and emotions. “I don’t feel necessarily very comfortable with acting. It’s not a place where I’m like, ‘I love it,’ but at the same time, I find it a bit painful. It’s more a way to experience things and ideas,” she says.

Gentle Monster provides another knotty premise, this time grounded in reality. It follows Lucy Weiss, a famed experimental pianist who gives up her career to raise her young son with her doting husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). One day, the police knock on their door, and it’s revealed that Philip is accused of distributing child pornography.

“The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” she says. “Things are not too defined, but it’s also because of the subject matter; it was the only way to talk about it. [Marie] gave me a lot of freedom.”

Born in Paris in 1985, Seydoux spent her childhood wedged among seven siblings and half-siblings. She remembers feeling isolated, practically invisible, as a child. “I was always in the shadows in a way,” she says. “As a kid, I felt transparent. I was never at the center of [my] big family.” This plays a big part in her surprise at her acting career, never thinking of herself as someone deserving of center stage, of fame, of attention. In fact, acting never even crossed her mind until she befriended some actors when she turned 18. “I loved the way they lived their lives,” she says. “I admired their life—the fact that they were free.”

And yet, she rejects the label of “actress.” “I try to be truthful as much as possible,” she says. “I try not to act. This is what I try to be, and I’m more into reaction than action.” She adds, “It’s more reactive than acting.”

For Seydoux, “reacting” and really experiencing life is only possible if she “abandons” herself to her characters. One of the most intense examples of this is Abdellatif Kechiche’s controversial lesbian love story Blue Is the Warmest Color, in which she stars alongside Adèle Exarchopoulos. The film’s schedule stretched from two months to five months, and it has since become notorious for its grueling pace. (The famous sex scene took 10 days alone.) “The greatest and most intense experience I’ve had was with Adèle when we did Blue Is the Warmest Color,” she recalls. “It was crazy, and really the connection between us had to be very, very intense. I think, with Adèle, it’s something that will always be a part of me now. There was an exchange, and it was more than making a film. It was really a life experience.”

One scene from Gentle Monster lingered with me for weeks after I watched it: Lucy’s snotty, tearful confrontation with her husband at their family home as she pleads with him to tell the truth. This close-up of Seydoux not only accentuates her famous blue eyes but also shows her uncanny control of this character’s emotions. It feels, for lack of better words, real. Seydoux partly credits her empathy to being a mother of two children, but this scene goes beyond compassion; it shows how much of herself she gives to the character. Before filming this scene, Kreutzer offered little direction, letting her tap into how the character felt. “I prepare myself, and I have to just let myself go and be in the moment and just open up,” Seydoux says, suggesting that perhaps these emotions otherwise lie dormant within her.

I found this is what Seydoux means by “reaction.” Every role in her varied and extolled filmography functions as an avenue through which she can explore what it means to be human, what it means to feel. Her natural curiosity about the world and its inhabitants existed before she decided to act in her teens and will exist long into the future, even if acting jobs don’t remain on the table. “If it ended tomorrow, I’d be happy,” she says. “I will find a way. Now, I really feel that I’m acting because I still have the opportunity. But I have more things to explore, and I would love to have more experiences.”

#6775. How to Read the Dune Book Series in Order

Our foolproof guide to getting caught up before Dune: Part Three hits theaters.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

So you're fired up about Dune: Part Three? The trilogy-capper is shaping up to be one of the biggest movies of the year, costarring just about every A-lister in Hollywood, including Timothée Chalamet, Zendaya, Robert Pattinson, and Florence Pugh. If you want to dive into the world of Frank Herbert's beloved novels before the film’s release on December 18, congratulations! You’ve come to the right place.

Dune is the world's best-selling science fiction novel. It's also considered one of the best books of all time. But whether you've dabbled in Dune lore before or you're completely new to the wild world of Arrakis, there's something for everyone in this Titanic-sized series about power, violence, and fate. You've got an exciting literary journey ahead.

Published in 1965, the book's massive success inspired Herbert to write several sequels, which brought the total number of novels in the series up to six when he died in 1986. The first Dune novel was split in half to cover both of Denis Villeneuve’s films so far, and the third novel, Dune: Messiah, will be adapted in Dune: Part Three. From there? Well, your guess is as good as mine.

Not only did Herbert write four more Dune novels, but his son Brian Herbert continued to write a little under two dozen more books in the Dune franchise following his father’s death. He teamed up with sci-fi writer Kevin J. Anderson to coauthor a trilogy of Dune prequels and then several spin-offs thereafter that filled in the gaps in Dune’s history.

But what's the right way to start your Dune journey? Well, there's no right answer. Some argue that the books should be read in the order of their fictive timeline, while others argue that they should be read in the order of publication.

Here's our advice: Read Frank Herbert's six novels first, then dabble in the spin-offs and prequels however you like. Given that many are grouped into smaller series within the larger story, you can sample bits and pieces of the universe. That's the beauty of Dune—it's a detailed series that rewards completionism, but the entry points are numerous.

Read on for a full breakdown of the books, listed here in the order in which they were published. Happy reading, spiceheads!—Josh Rosenberg

Dune is set far in the future, in an intergalactic feudal society where powerful noble houses fight for control over resources, armies, and planetary power. House Atreides is ordered to take control of Arrakis, a barren desert planet with a brutal climate. Arrakis is the only place to mine melange, a natural resource that produces a psychoactive drug called spice, which allows human beings to unlock their minds. On Arrakis, House Atreides is betrayed by its rival House Harkonnen, which sets off a battle over the valuable planet. The planet itself is inhabited by giant sandworms and a native population known as Fremen, who, over the generations, have learned to survive with water as their most precious resource and currency. When House Atreides scion Paul is targeted as a potential messiah to lead the planet—and galaxy—toward a new era, an epic story of war, betrayal, and mysticism unfolds. Cinephiles, this one is a must: Denis Villeneuve's Dune and Dune: Part Two span this book.

In Herbert's first sequel, Paul Atreides, now known as Muad'Dib, rules the known universe as the most powerful emperor of all time. Worshipped as a messiah by the people of Arrakis, Paul faces enmity from the warring political houses under his control. Is any one ruler meant to have such absolute power? In Dune Messiah, Herbert unmakes his previous novel, all while warning, "No more terrible disaster could befall your people than for them to fall into the hands of a hero." Film fans, don't miss this one—you'll want to be caught up when Villeneuve's Dune Part Three lands in theaters on December 19, 2026.

Children of Dune picks up with Leto and Ghanima Atreides, the twin children of Paul Atreides, nine years after their father's mysterious disappearance into the wastelands of Arrakis. The twins' prophetic abilities are coveted by their manipulative aunt Alia, who rules the Empire, but these two young prophets refuse to be anyone's pawns.

Thirty-five hundred years after the events of Children of Dune, the once-desert planet of Arrakis is now a lush paradise, and Leto Atreides sits on the throne. Millennia ago, Leto merged with a sandworm to grant himself immortality, but the cost to his humanity has been enormous. Can a rebellion led by Siona, a rival relative, unseat this fearsome despot?

Leto Atreides is dead, Arrakis (now called Rakis) is once again a desert wasteland, and the Empire has fallen into ruin. A young girl named Sheeana seems to fulfill a prophecy foretold by the late God Emperor, sending religious fervor through the galaxy. Is Sheeana destined to return the Empire to its former glory?

In Frank Herbert's final Dune novel, Arrakis has been destroyed, and the fate of the Empire rests in the hands of a mysterious matriarchal order known as the Bene Gesserit. On the planet Chapterhouse, the sisters are breeding sandworms and seeking to control spice production, with the goal of remaking the galaxy for a brighter future.

Brian Herbert (Frank Herbert’s son) and Kevin J. Anderson's Prelude to Dune trilogy begins with this story of the generation before the one in Dune—namely, that of Leto Atreides, father of Paul. In House Atreides, we see how Leto's rivalries and relationships sowed the catalyzing events of Dune.

In House Harkonnen, Leto Atreides's longtime rival, the Baron Vladimir Harkonnen, comes into view. We also meet Abulurd Rabban, brother and foil to the baron. Turns out, there are good people in House Harkonnen—who knew?

In House Corrino, Herbert and Anderson conclude their prequel series, bringing the story up to the climactic events set to unfold in Dune. This tapestry of politics, warmongering, and spice battles ends with the birth of Paul Atreides, preparing us for the saga we know and love (and now have already read).

Working from Frank Herbert's notes for a "Dune 7," his son and Kevin J. Anderson again expanded the series in Legends of Dune, a new trilogy. The first installment, The Butlerian Jihad, digs into an event Herbert often referred to but never captured at scale: the long-ago war in which humans fought for their freedom from "the thinking machines." Set 10,000 years before Dune, this volume sees the familiar chess pieces come into view.

The Legends of Dune trilogy continues with The Machine Crusade, set two decades after The Butlerian Jihad. The thinking machines fight back, refusing to go quietly into that good night. Meanwhile, on Arrakis, a band of outlaws take their first steps to becoming the Fremen, a race of people that OG Dune fans know and love.

Check out that sandworm on the cover! The Legends of Dune trilogy concludes with The Battle of Corrin, which tees up a final apocalyptic showdown between humans and robots. Fans of Dune know how this one ends, but it sure is fun to see how Herbert and Anderson get there.

Dune Sequels, a two-volume spin-off series, concludes the storyline from Herbert's six original novels, with insight from a long-lost outline found hidden in one of the author's safety deposit boxes. In Hunters of Dune, we pick up with the escaping fugitives last seen at the end of Chapterhouse: Dune as they strengthen their powers and fight for the future of the human race.

Herbert and Anderson answer more burning questions in this second volume of the Dune Sequels series—namely, the future of Arrakis and the outcome of the war between man and machine.

Set between Dune and Dune Messiah, the Heroes of Dune spin-off series opens with a tale of Paul Atreides. Dune ends with Paul ruling Arrakis, while Dune Messiah opens with him ruling the galaxy. Just how did he gain control of the Empire? Read Paul of Dune to find out.

Heroes of Dune continues with this second and final installment, set after the events of Dune Messiah. The Winds of Dune picks up after Paul Atreides's disappearance into the Arrakis desert, leaving the Empire in crisis and the line of succession in question. Who will hold everything together?

The Great Schools of Dune trilogy opens with Sisterhood of Dune, set almost a century after the game-changing Battle of Corrin. With the thinking machines destroyed, political and religious movements rise, setting the stage for an epic conflict between reason and faith. You'll want to read this one to understand more about HBO's Dune: Prophecy series.

In Book Two of the Schools of Dune Trilogy, the thinking machines are gone, but a new school opens to teach humans the efficient techniques of those machines. The Butlerian jihadists staunchly oppose any machinist way of life, however, and pick a dangerous fight with the Mentat School. What ensues is a showdown for humanity's future, with the risk of a potential dark age.

Want to learn even more about the Bene Gesserit sisterhood from Herbert's original novels? Then Navigators of Dune is the book for you. In this third and final volume of the Great Schools of Dune trilogy, we learn about the origins of the Bene Gesserit and see their secretive way of life develop.

The latest trilogy from Herbert and Anderson, The Caladan Trilogy, begins with The Duke of Caladan, a prequel about the life of Leto Atreides. Just how did the ruler of a quiet planet become such a power player in a galactically fateful story? If that's the question on your mind, this is the book you ought to hit next.

Herbert and Anderson take it all the way back to OG Dune in this prequel about Lady Jessica, mother of Paul Atreides. In The Lady of Caladan, Lady Jessica reckons with her choice to betray the Bene Gesserit, her ancient maternal order, while destiny converges around her husband and son.

Have you read everything up to this point about Paul Atreides but you still can't get enough of the guy? We've got a book for you. The Caladan Trilogy concludes with The Heir of Caladan, a deep dive into the young heir's metamorphosis from teenager to messiah.

Set two years before the events of Dune, Princess of Dune delves into the lives of the two most important women in Paul Atreides's life: his wife, Princess Irulan, and his true love, Chani. Though these characters are often at odds in their separate quests to influence Paul, Princess of Dune draws striking parallels between these brave, ambitious, and embattled young women. Through their expanded backstory, one comes to see the events of Dune in a new light.

Superfans will love this collection of novellas, each one taking place in an under-explored corner of the Dune-verse. Just what was Gurney Halleck up to during his lost years working undercover as an Arrakis smuggler? What's it like to be one of the fearsome Sardaukar? How did Shadout Mapes go from a Fremen freedom fighter to an imperial housekeeper? If you've ever asked these questions, you'll find your answers in Sands of Dune.

The Road to Dune contains short stories, previously unpublished works, and correspondence from the late Frank Herbert that all led to the creation of Dune. Plus, you can read the novella “Spice Planet,” based on a detailed outline left by Frank Herbert.

#6774. Christy Turlington’s Ruby-Red Lipstick Is the Easy Makeup Secret for Women Over 40

Shop our favorite creamy, hydrating, and smoothing lipstick formulas

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last few years have been marked by the “Lip Balm Wars.” That is, every brand—from Hailey Bieber’s Rhode to prestige labels like Chanel and Dior—has launched more sheer, glossy, and hydrating formulas than classic lipstick bullets. But even though we all own more than 10 emotional-support lip balms in various shades, flavors, and finishes, there's still nothing more powerful than a statement-making lip. This is especially true for women over 40.

Last night, supermodel Christy Turlington stepped out at the Museum of Modern Art’s 2026 Annual Film Benefit in New York, wearing a knit dress from Matthieu Blazy’s fall 2026 Chanel collection. A dress this graphic and eye-catching needs makeup to balance it, and Turlington paired it with black eyeliner and a glossy ruby-red lipstick.

While we don’t know the exact shade she wore last night, it’s clearly a formula that sits somewhere between a satin lipstick and a gloss-balm, with pigment that doesn’t sacrifice moisture or shine. Turlington is a Lancôme ambassador, so it could be the brand’s Lip Idole Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm or L’Absolu Rouge Cream Lipstick. Otherwise, Chanel makes a gorgeous option in both the Rouge Coco Flash and Rouge Allure Extrait formulas. These formulas subtly plump and moisturize dry lips while blurring lip lines and imperfections.

Lancôme L'Absolu Rouge Moisturizing Cream Lipstick in 397 Berry Noir

Chanel Hydrating Vibrant Shine Lip Colour

Lancome Lip Idole Squalane-12 Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm

The best part of this look is that it proves you don’t need to match your lipstick exactly to your outfit, as long as they’re in the same color family. While the Chanel dress is more of an orange-red knit, Turlington’s lipstick is a more neutral ruby that harmonizes with the warm tones and shades in the rest of her look. Plus, raspberry or ruby-red lipstick can brighten your face in seconds, even with little to no other makeup.

Bold lipstick is a bit outside Turlington’s day-to-day comfort zone; she told Bazaar recently that she’s “definitely an old-school Carmex or ChapStick kind of person, because I’m constantly reapplying it to my dry lips, especially in the winter.” The rest of her routine is equally as effortless: “I wash my face in the morning and moisturize a lot. Sometimes I even double-moisturize because, especially at this time of year, I get dry skin. And then, I’ll add a nice little layer of foundation, some blush, and mascara, and that’s kind of it, I’m on my way.”

#6771. Is This a ‘Gut Feeling’ About My Relationship... or Just My Anxiety?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

We’re often told that we should trust our gut in relationships—that when something is wrong, we’ll get a distinct feeling in our bodies. But what if that feeling isn’t distinct at all? What if these feelings of dread and alarm are constant? What if you can’t really trust your gut?

I’ve had anxiety for as long as I can remember. And when I get into relationships, I feel the symptoms tenfold. I create worst-case scenarios in my head, over-analyze every little reaction, and go into a panic when a partner has the slightest shift in attitude. A lot of this anxiety has to do with my past (surprise, surprise). I was in an emotionally abusive relationship, riddled with infidelity and manipulation, for six years. And although it’s been years since my breakup and I’ve moved on to an entirely new relationship that feels healthy and fulfilling, I don’t think my body and brain have quite gotten the memo.

“A person who has experienced neglect, abandonment, unpredictability or inconsistencies in past relationships may be unknowingly conditioned to scan for threat, even in a healthy, stable relationship,” Jessica N. Gordon, LCSW tells Cosmopolitan.

So if you come equipped with intense relational trauma, how do you know the difference between when something is actually wrong in your relationship—and when it’s just your anxiety acting up again?

When every little thing feels like the end of the world, it becomes hard to discern when there’s a real concern in your relationship. This is typically what Relationship Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (ROCD) looks like.

"Anxiety driven by ROCD tends to feel very intense and urgent,” Cameron Murphey, LMFT says. “It feels like you have to address it right now.” He explains that people with ROCD typically feel like they’re unable to focus on anything else until they've addressed what's making them feel anxious.

But the problem is what’s making them anxious isn’t exactly rooted in something concrete. People with ROCD constantly ruminate on thoughts like “How do I know I’m truly in love?,” “There have been no signs, but I think they’re cheating on me,” or “Is my partner doing enough?” There might not even be a specific action that triggers these thoughts.

“An [ROCD] thought tends to jump from topic to topic... and feels spontaneous rather than a consistent subtle thought,” Gordon says. A true concern in the relationship, on the other hand, is tied to real, observable behavior that shows up in a pattern: “every time we talk about my feelings, he picks up his phone and I feel ignored.”

But what if your gut is just screaming at you that something is wrong?

“I think we sometimes give our gut feelings a lot of authority without stopping to consider what's informing them,” Sonni Williams, LPC says. “Our intuition can be shaped by our experiences, values, and even the things we're afraid of repeating.”

She adds that we sometimes tend to oversimplify intuition as calm and anxiety as panic. The problem with this is that both of these things can feel convincing, and neither feeling alone tells us whether something is true.

Rather than torturing yourself with these thoughts as they come, Williams says it’s more effective and helpful to look at what happens afterward.

“Can you reflect on the concern and decide whether something needs attention?” she says to ask yourself. “Or are you repeatedly analyzing conversations and feelings, trying to find certainty?”

Now this doesn’t mean you need an immediate answer in order to take your feelings seriously. Williams explains that when it comes to concerns involving abuse, coercion, or controlling behaviors, these things deserve attention regardless of whether anxiety or ROCD are present.

It’s absolutely a good thing to reflect on your relationship and assess if it’s working for you. But Williams explains that what’s most important is knowing the difference between evaluating your relationship and feeling like you have to constantly prove that it’s right or perfect.

And you may not be able to trust your gut, specifically, but that doesn’t mean you shouldn’t trust yourself—you just have to learn what that looks like in the context of your anxiety.

"Self-trust is not about believing that you'll always make the right choice,” Murphey says. “It's about trusting that you can handle whatever happens, good or bad.”

He explains that we tend to feel fear in our stomachs, so it’s easy to confuse those very physical feelings with something grander like intuition. And guess what? That’s totally fine—so long as you don’t use those feelings to solely dictate your actions in your relationship. If you get those feelings, think about why or where they’re coming from.

You’re going to have second thoughts about your partner from time-to-time (or if you’re me, every three business days). Seeing a therapist for lingering, uncontrollable thoughts can help you work through whatever anxieties you may have. Otherwise, there are going to be times where you just inevitably question your relationship.

Gordon says, “The goal is to feel less controlled by doubt, not to never have it.”
